The Interim Government’s experience demonstrated the structural limits of a power‑sharing arrangement without a mutually accepted constitutional framework — a lesson that informed the design of the post‑independence Indian Cabinet system.

The Role of the All‑India Students Federation and Youth Mobilisation in the Final Phase of the Freedom Struggle (1936‑1947)

The All‑India Students Federation (AISF), founded in August 1936 at Lucknow, became the principal organisational vehicle for radical student politics in the late colonial period. Its early leadership, including Prem Narayan Bhargava, articulated a programme that combined anti‑imperialism with demands for educational reform, fee concessions, and the release of political prisoners. The AISF’s growing affiliation with the Communist Party of India gave it access to a cadre network that extended into trade unions and kisan sabhas, enabling coordinated actions across campuses and factories.

During the Second World War, the AISF’s stance shifted with the Communist Party’s line: it supported the war effort after 1941 and did not participate in the Quit India Movement. In the 1945‑46 INA trials agitation, however, AISF volunteers formed “defence committees” in Delhi, Lahore, and Calcutta, mobilising mass rallies demanding the release of INA officers. The Federation’s pamphlets argued that the trials were a test of Indian sovereignty, linking the student movement to the broader nationalist narrative. When the Royal Indian Navy mutiny erupted in February 1946, AISF activists in Bombay and Karachi organised solidarity strikes in colleges, providing food and medical aid to mutineers.

In the 1946 provincial elections, the AISF campaigned for Communist Party candidates and for Congress‑Left alliances, securing a modest but visible presence in student unions across Punjab, Bengal, and the United Provinces. After partition, the Federation split along communal lines: the Pakistan branch reorganised as the Democratic Students Federation, while the Indian branch continued as AISF, later affiliating with the World Federation of Democratic Youth. The AISF’s trajectory illustrates how a youth organisation, rooted in a specific ideological current, could amplify mass movements, bridge the gap between intellectual dissent and street mobilisation, and leave a lasting imprint on post‑colonial student politics.

  • Confusing the Defence of India Act 1915 with the Rowlatt Act 1919 — the former was a wartime measure, the latter a peacetime extension.
  • Misstating the duration of Gandhi’s 1932 fast (21 days, not 118) or overlooking the simultaneous negotiation with Ambedkar that produced the Poona Pact.
  • Assuming the Partition Council divided assets purely on population ratios; the actual formula combined population, revenue contribution, and strategic considerations.
  • Overlooking the Viceroy’s retained control over Defence in the Interim Government, which prevented Patel from fully commanding the police during communal riots.
  • Treating the AISF as a monolithic Congress front; it was organisationally tied to the Communist Party and often at odds with the Congress student wing.
  • Neglecting the role of the “War Book” classification in the 1939 censorship regime, which created a tiered press control absent in 1915.
  • Attributing Quit India participation to the AISF; the Communist Party opposed the movement, so the AISF did not lead student protests in 1942.
  • Review these traps carefully before the exam.

The Role of the Congress Working Committee in Shaping the 1946‑47 Negotiations

The Congress Working Committee (CWC) functioned as the principal executive body of the Indian National Congress, and its influence peaked during the critical negotiations of 1946‑47. By mid‑1946 the CWC included Jawaharlal Nehru (Congress President), Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, Maulana Abul Kalam Azad, and Jagjivan Ram. Their collective decisions shaped the Congress stance on the Cabinet Mission, the Interim Government, and the eventual acceptance of the Mountbatten Plan.

Strategic deliberations in July 1946 saw the CWC endorse the Cabinet Mission’s “grouping” formula while recording reservations, notably from Patel, about provincial autonomy. The committee’s discussions reflected a tension between leaders favouring a strong centre (Nehru, Azad) and those concerned about regional balance (Patel, Ram). This internal debate informed the Congress’s public rejection of separate electorates, a position that hardened the League’s demand for a separate state.

Negotiating positions were refined through informal consultations between Nehru and Patel in late 1946. They emphasised three conditions for any transfer of power: a clear Congress majority in the Constituent Assembly, unified control of defence and foreign affairs, and minority safeguards through constitutional guarantees rather than separate electorates. These points were conveyed to the British delegation and fed into the discussions that produced the June 1947 Plan.

Engagement with the Muslim League involved direct talks between Patel and Jinnah in late 1946, exploring a joint declaration for a united India. Jinnah’s insistence on the “two‑nation” principle ended these efforts, underscoring the CWC’s limited leverage over communal politics. The failure of dialogue pushed the League to articulate its demand for Pakistan more explicitly, culminating in its acceptance of the June 1947 Plan as a political counter‑move.

In June 1947 the All‑India Congress Committee (AICC), acting on the CWC’s recommendation, approved the “June 3 Plan” (partition with a mid‑August transfer date) by a large majority. This decision gave the Interim Government the political mandate to proceed, and the British Parliament enacted the Indian Independence Act shortly after. The CWC’s internal dynamics thus directly influenced the constitutional settlement and the timeline of the transfer of power.

Communal Violence as a Political Tool: The 1946 Direct Action Day and Its Aftermath

Direct Action Day, called by the All‑India Muslim League for 16 August 1946, was intended as a mass demonstration of Muslim political resolve. Although the League described it as a “peaceful protest,” the widespread violence that erupted in Calcutta and spread to other provinces showed how communal mobilisation could alter political calculations.

Planning and mobilisation began weeks earlier when the League’s Working Committee circulated instructions to provincial units urging “maximum participation” and the “display of Muslim unity.” In Bengal, the provincial League under Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy coordinated with student organisations to mobilise volunteers. Contemporary accounts indicate that some volunteers were armed with sticks and other improvised weapons.

Trigger events in Calcutta followed a large League rally. The police presence was widely criticised as inadequate for a city of over four million, and the initial clashes escalated into three days of rioting. The Bengal Provincial Commission of Inquiry (1947) estimated roughly 4,000 deaths.

Political consequences were immediate. Congress leaders cited the Calcutta violence as evidence that a united India was untenable. In September 1946 Assembly debates, Congress representatives described the riots as proof of the League’s willingness to use force for political ends. British records from August‑September 1946 note that the League’s capacity to incite disorder had become a factor in settlement planning.

Beyond Calcutta, similar patterns appeared in the Noakhali riots (October 1946) and the Bihar massacres (December 1946), where local League committees issued calls for “defence of Muslim homes.” Historians such as Bipan Chandra argue these coordinated outbreaks served a dual purpose: pressuring the British to concede a separate state and forcing the Congress into a harder line, narrowing the space for compromise.

The violence also spurred the formation of Hindu and Sikh “self‑defence” groups, notably Akali volunteer units in Punjab. Initially reactive, some adopted offensive tactics, fuelling a cycle of retaliation that further destabilised the environment. By the time the Mountbatten Plan was announced in June 1947, communal violence had become an entrenched element in the bargaining process, compelling the British to treat partition as a pragmatic necessity.

Technique: Linking Primary Sources to Answer Framing
Explain how the Bengal Provincial Commission of Inquiry’s casualty figures are used to assess the impact of Direct Action Day on the Congress’s negotiating position.

Begin with a brief description of the commission (established in March 1947). State its official estimate: approximately 4,000 deaths and 10,000 injuries. Connect the magnitude of loss to the Congress narrative: the high death toll was cited in the Congress’s September 1946 Assembly debates as evidence of the League’s “willingness to employ terror.” Note that British Cabinet records from late 1946 explicitly reference the commission’s findings when “re‑evaluating the feasibility of a united dominion.” Conclude by observing that the casualty data provided an empirical basis for the Congress to argue that partition was unavoidable, strengthening its position in the final negotiations.

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “the role of communal violence in the 1946 negotiations,” start with the League’s Direct Action Day as the pivot, then trace the chain of subsequent riots, cite official casualty figures, and finish with the British administrative response. Use the chronological anchor (August 1946) to structure your answer and avoid drifting into generic “partition violence” discussions.

The Legal Architecture of Partition: The Indian Independence Act, 1947 and Its Constitutional Implications

The Indian Independence Act (IIA) received royal assent on 18 July 1947 and came into force on 15 August 1947. A concise statute of 20 sections, it dissolved British suzerainty and created two independent dominions, India and Pakistan. Its brevity, however, left several constitutional questions unresolved.

Section 1 established the two dominions and terminated the Crown‑India relationship, transferring legislative sovereignty to the respective dominion legislatures. The British Parliament retained the power to amend the Act, a provision that remained technically operative until each dominion enacted its own constitution.

Section 9 constituted the Partition Council to divide assets and liabilities based on population, revenue contribution, and strategic considerations. The Council’s report (September 1947) allocated the railway network, financial assets, and military stores using this mixed formula; strategic factors such as control of key Punjab railway junctions were explicitly weighed.

Section 8 recognised the existing Constituent Assemblies as the sovereign law‑making bodies for each dominion, authorising them to frame constitutions “as soon as practicable.” This clause provided the legal basis for the Indian Constituent Assembly (elected 1946) and its Pakistani counterpart to function as fully sovereign legislatures.

Section 7 placed the armed forces under a transitional command structure: the British Commander‑in‑Chief retained operational control for a brief period, enabling British officers to assist during the communal crises of late 1947.

Constitutionally, the IIA created “dominions” that were internationally sovereign yet remained within the Commonwealth. This dual status persisted until India adopted its Constitution on 26 January 1950, superseding the IIA, while Pakistan retained the Act’s framework until its 1956 Constitution. In exam answers, emphasise that the IIA was a transfer instrument, not a comprehensive constitution; issues of citizenship, minority rights, and princely states were left to the subsequent constitutional processes (India’s Articles 5‑11, Pakistan’s Objectives Resolution of 1949).

Radcliffe Commission Methodology and the Contested Delimitation of Boundaries

The Boundary Commissions, chaired by Sir Cyril Radcliffe, were tasked with demarcating the borders of Punjab and Bengal. Radcliffe, a British jurist without prior Indian experience, had roughly five weeks to produce an award acceptable to both the Congress and the Muslim League.

Data collection drew on the 1941 Census, 1946 electoral rolls, and land‑revenue records. The data were incomplete: many villages in western Punjab lacked current revenue figures due to wartime disruption, and the 1941 Census did not reflect population shifts caused by the 1943 famine.

Methodological criteria were: (i) religious majority, (ii) contiguity, and (iii) economic viability (irrigation, railways, ports). Radcliffe gave primacy to religious majority but compromised on economic viability where infrastructure needs dictated, notably in Gurdaspur district, where the award to India provided a land link to Jammu & Kashmir.

Decision‑making relied on brief on‑the‑ground inspections by small teams of British and Indian officials. In Bengal, teams visited a limited number of towns, relying heavily on local informants whose testimony often reflected communal bias. The final award (17 August 1947) allocated districts in Punjab and Bengal based on these assessments.

Contested outcomes emerged immediately. In Punjab, the division split Lahore’s hinterland, stranding minorities on both sides. In Bengal, Murshidabad (Muslim‑majority) was awarded to India citing economic ties to Calcutta, sparking protests. Legal challenges in both dominions were dismissed; courts held the award was an executive act under the IIA not subject to judicial review.

Analytically, the Radcliffe exercise shows how “technical demarcation” was overridden by political imperatives. Reliance on flawed data, an impossible deadline, and the absence of a neutral arbiter produced a boundary that, while legally sanctioned, seeded enduring tensions and massive displacement—a key point for any answer on the causes of Partition violence.

  • Confusing the CWC’s internal discussions on the Cabinet Mission with the AICC’s formal vote on the June 3 Plan; the latter was the decisive vote on partition.
  • Presenting Direct Action Day as a spontaneous outbreak rather than a coordinated political mobilisation; the League’s pre‑planned circular is well documented.
  • Stating that the Indian Independence Act provided a detailed constitutional framework; it was a transfer instrument leaving key questions unresolved.
  • Claiming the Radcliffe Commission used only census data; it also used electoral rolls, revenue records, and local informants, introducing bias.
  • Attributing the Gurdaspur award solely to religious majority; strategic access to Kashmir was a decisive factor.
  • Assuming the Partition Council’s asset division was purely population‑based; the formula combined population, revenue, and strategic factors.
  • Overlooking that the IIA permitted the British Parliament to amend it, a provision relevant until each dominion enacted its own constitution.

The 1945‑46 General Elections: Electoral Politics, Mandate, and the Road to Partition

The general elections of December 1945 to January 1946 were the first held under the Government of India Act 1935 after the war‑time suspension of provincial autonomy. They covered both the Central Legislative Assembly and the provincial legislatures, providing a concrete measure of popular support for the major political formations. The Indian National Congress contested on a platform of complete independence and a united India, while the All‑India Muslim League campaigned explicitly for the creation of Pakistan on the basis of the Lahore Resolution. The results revealed a sharp communal divide: Congress secured a decisive majority in the general seats of the Central Assembly and formed ministries in eight of the eleven provinces, including the key provinces of Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar, Central Provinces, Orissa, Assam and the North‑West Frontier Province. The Muslim League captured nearly all Muslim seats in the Central Assembly and won clear majorities in the Muslim‑reserved constituencies of Bengal, Punjab and Sind, though it fell short of a majority in the Punjab provincial assembly, necessitating a coalition with the Unionist Party.

The electoral outcome gave both parties a claim to a popular mandate. Congress interpreted its sweeping victory in non‑Muslim constituencies as a mandate for a united, sovereign India, while the League argued that its near‑total capture of Muslim seats demonstrated the Muslim community’s desire for a separate state. The British government, now under a Labour administration committed to a rapid transfer of power, used the election results to justify the Cabinet Mission’s grouping scheme and, ultimately, the June 3 Plan. The elections also exposed the limits of the 1935 Act’s federal design: the separate electorate system, retained from the 1919 reforms, amplified communal polarisation and made the formation of a unified national government practically impossible. In the months following the polls, the Interim Government was constituted on the basis of the election arithmetic, but the deep mistrust between Congress and the League rendered the cabinet dysfunctional, accelerating the move toward partition.

For examiners, the 1945‑46 elections are a pivotal link between the constitutional negotiations of 1946 and the final partition decision. Answers should connect the electoral arithmetic to the Cabinet Mission’s grouping proposals, the failure of the Interim Government, and the British decision to advance the transfer date. Emphasise that the elections were not a referendum on partition per se, but the League’s ability to monopolise Muslim representation gave it the political leverage to demand a separate state.

The Indian Civil Service and the Transfer of Power: Continuity, Reorganisation, and the Making of the Post‑Colonial Bureaucracy

The Indian Civil Service (ICS) was the steel frame of British administration in India. By 1945 it comprised approximately 1,200 officers, predominantly British. The transfer of power negotiations explicitly addressed the future of this cadre. The 1947 Indian Independence Act provided for the continuation of existing services under the new dominions, but the political leadership of both India and Pakistan insisted on rapid Indianisation. In India, the Constituent Assembly’s Advisory Committee on the Services recommended the creation of the Indian Administrative Service (IAS) and the Indian Police Service (IPS) as all‑India services, preserving the unified recruitment and career structure that the ICS had embodied. The first IAS batch was recruited in 1948 through a competitive examination conducted by the Union Public Service Commission, modelled on the old ICS entrance test.

Continuity was evident in the retention of senior British officers during the transitional period. The last British ICS officers served in key positions until 1950, ensuring administrative stability during the communal crisis and the integration of princely states. In Pakistan, the Civil Service of Pakistan (CSP) was constituted on similar lines, with the first batch drawn from the existing ICS officers who opted for Pakistan. The division of personnel was governed by the Partition Council’s formula, which allocated officers on the basis of domicile, option, and the needs of the new governments. Approximately 300 ICS officers chose Pakistan, while the remainder stayed in India.

The reorganisation also involved the abolition of the “steel frame” rhetoric and the introduction of a merit‑based, politically neutral civil service mandated by the new constitutions. Article 310 of the Indian Constitution (later Article 311) guaranteed security of tenure, while the All‑India Services Act 1951 provided the legal framework for the IAS and IPS. The legacy of the ICS persists in the district collector’s role, the hierarchical secretariat system, and the emphasis on generalist administration. Understanding this continuity is essential for answers on post‑independence governance, the integration of princely states, and the administrative challenges of refugee rehabilitation.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Explain how the 1945‑46 elections influenced the Cabinet Mission’s grouping scheme.” A common error is to describe the 1937 provincial elections instead of the 1945‑46 central and provincial polls.
The 1945‑46 elections were held after the war and produced a clear communal split: Congress won the general seats, the League swept Muslim seats. The Cabinet Mission used this arithmetic to propose three groups of provinces (A, B, C) reflecting the election results, aiming to accommodate League’s demand for Muslim‑majority groupings while preserving a weak centre. The 1937 elections, by contrast, had given Congress ministries in most provinces and had not produced a League mandate for Pakistan. Therefore, the correct answer links the 1945‑46 electoral outcome directly to the grouping scheme, not the 1937 results.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for “electoral mandate” or “popular mandate” in the 1945‑47 period, always specify which election (central vs provincial, 1945‑46 vs 1937) and which community’s seats are being referenced. Use a two‑column table in your rough notes: Congress – general seats; League – Muslim seats. This prevents the common mix‑up between the 1937 and 1945‑46 polls.

The Division of the Indian Armed Forces and the Joint Defence Council: Negotiations, Asset Allocation, and Operational Challenges

The partition of British India required the division of one of the largest volunteer armies in the world, numbering over two million personnel at the end of World War II. The Partition Council, chaired by the Viceroy and later by the Governor‑General of each dominion, established a Joint Defence Council (JDC) to oversee the split. The JDC comprised the Commanders‑in‑Chief of the two new armies, the British Commander‑in‑Chief (who remained in a transitional role), and representatives of the defence ministries. The guiding principle was “equitable distribution” based on the ratio of personnel opting for each dominion, the location of units, and strategic considerations such as the defence of the new borders.

Approximately 260,000 officers and men opted for Pakistan, while the remainder stayed with India. The division of equipment followed a formula that combined population share (roughly 80:20), revenue contribution, and the strategic location of depots. Key assets such as the artillery school at Deolali, the armoured corps centre at Ahmednagar, and the air force training establishments at Ambala and Risalpur were allocated to India, while Pakistan received the ordnance factories at Rawalpindi and the naval dockyard at Karachi. The British government supplied additional equipment to both dominions under the “Military Aid” programme to compensate for shortfalls.

Operational challenges were immediate. The communal violence of August‑September 1947 disrupted troop movements, and the newly formed armies had to be deployed for internal security as well as border defence. The JDC functioned until April 1948, when the two dominions assumed full command. The experience of the division shaped the early defence doctrines of both states, influencing the 1948‑49 Kashmir war and the subsequent institutionalisation of separate defence ministries. For exam answers, stress the role of the JDC as a temporary mechanism, the use of the “opt‑in” principle for personnel, and the strategic logic behind the allocation of key installations.

The Federal Court under the 1935 Act: Jurisdiction, Key Judgments, and Its Legacy for the Supreme Court of India

The Government of India Act 1935 established the Federal Court of India, which began functioning on 1 October 1937. It was the first judicial body with explicit federal jurisdiction in the subcontinent, possessing original, appellate, and advisory functions. Original jurisdiction extended to disputes between the Centre and provinces or between provinces inter se, appellate jurisdiction covered civil and criminal appeals from the High Courts, and advisory jurisdiction allowed the Governor‑General to refer questions of law. The Court consisted of a Chief Justice and up to six puisne judges, appointed by the Crown.

During its decade of existence, the Federal Court delivered several landmark rulings that defined the constitutional relationship between the Centre and the provinces. It upheld the validity of the Defence of India Act’s emergency provisions, affirming the Centre’s legislative competence over defence and external affairs. In an advisory opinion, the Court clarified that the residuary legislative powers rested with the Centre, a principle later incorporated into Article 248 of the Indian Constitution. The Court also adjudicated on the interpretation of the “instrument of accession” for princely states, providing a legal framework that the post‑independence government used during integration.

The Federal Court’s jurisprudence directly informed the design of the Supreme Court of India under the 1950 Constitution. The Supreme Court inherited the Federal Court’s original jurisdiction (Article 131), appellate jurisdiction (Articles 132‑134), and advisory jurisdiction (Article 143). Many Federal Court judges, including Sir H. J. Kania, became the first judges of the Supreme Court, ensuring continuity of judicial reasoning. Understanding the Federal Court’s role is essential for answers on the evolution of judicial review, the federal balance, and the constitutional continuity between the 1935 Act and the 1950 Constitution.

  • Confusing the 1945‑46 central elections with the 1937 provincial elections when discussing the mandate for partition.
  • Assuming the Indian Civil Service was wholly replaced at independence; in reality, a large British cadre remained until 1950.
  • Overlooking the Joint Defence Council’s temporary nature and attributing the final division of assets solely to the Partition Council.
  • Stating that the Federal Court had the power of judicial review over legislation; it could only interpret the 1935 Act, not strike down laws.
  • Neglecting the “opt‑in” principle for armed‑forces personnel and treating the division as a purely territorial split.
  • Mixing up the advisory jurisdiction of the Federal Court with that of the Supreme Court; the former was invoked by the Governor‑General, the latter by the President.
  • Presenting the 1945‑46 election results as a direct referendum on Pakistan, rather than a communal seat‑count that the League leveraged politically.

These advanced sections integrate constitutional, administrative, and military dimensions of the final phase of the freedom struggle, providing the analytical depth required for high‑scoring answers.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Railway Network

The partition of British India required a rapid and complex division of the railway system, which had been the backbone of colonial economic integration. The Railway Board, operating under the Government of India Act 1935, controlled a network of approximately 65,000 route kilometres, thousands of stations and a large rolling stock fleet. The Partition Council, chaired by Lord Mountbatten, established a Railway Sub‑Committee in mid‑1947 to allocate assets on the basis of geographical location, traffic density and anticipated revenue. The broad principle adopted was a territorial split: lines lying wholly within the new dominions were transferred outright, while inter‑dominion trunk routes — such as the North‑Western Railway's Lahore‑Delhi‑Calcutta corridor — were placed under a joint management arrangement for a transitional period.

Operationally, the division created immediate bottlenecks. The sudden loss of the North‑Western Railway's major workshops at Mughalpura (Lahore) and the locomotive shed at Rawalpindi deprived India of heavy maintenance capacity, while Pakistan lost the major carriage‑building facility at Perambur (Madras) and signalling workshops at Howrah. To mitigate the disruption, the British government supplied additional locomotives and wagons under a military aid programme, and a temporary Railway Board for Pakistan was constituted in Karachi under the leadership of a former North‑Western Railway General Manager. In India, the Railway Board continued in Delhi, but the loss of the strategic Khyber Pass route forced a re‑orientation of freight traffic toward the Bombay‑Delhi‑Calcutta axis.

The administrative reorganisation was formalised by the Indian Independence (Railways) Order 1947, which vested all railway assets in the respective dominion governments and provided for the settlement of outstanding liabilities — pensions, provident fund balances and contractual obligations — through a joint financial committee. The committee, meeting between August 1947 and early 1948, adopted a formula that combined route‑kilometre share (approximately 80:20 in India's favour), revenue contribution (based on 1945‑46 earnings) and the location of fixed assets. The final settlement, ratified by the Partition Council in April 1948, transferred the bulk of route kilometres to India and the remainder to Pakistan, together with a proportionate share of rolling stock, workshops and signalling equipment.

The long‑term impact of this division shaped the developmental trajectory of both railway systems. India embarked on a massive gauge‑conversion programme in the 1950s, standardising on broad gauge (1,676 mm) and integrating the erstwhile princely state railways through the 1951 Railway Zones scheme. Pakistan, constrained by a narrower asset base, retained a mixed gauge network (broad, metre and narrow) for longer and only began large‑scale conversion decades later. For examination answers, emphasise the role of the Railway Sub‑Committee, the territorial‑asset principle, the temporary joint management of inter‑dominion routes, and the financial settlement formula that combined kilometre‑share, revenue and fixed‑asset location.

The Division of Financial Assets and the Reserve Bank of India in 1947

The partition of financial assets was arguably the most technically demanding aspect of the 1947 transfer of power. The Reserve Bank of India (RBI), established in 1935 as the central banking authority for the whole of British India, held the monopoly of note issue, managed the foreign exchange reserves, and acted as banker to the central and provincial governments. The Partition Council appointed a Finance Sub‑Committee, chaired by Sir Chintaman Deshmukh (then Deputy Governor of the RBI), to devise a division mechanism that would preserve monetary stability in both dominions.

The committee adopted a three‑tier approach. First, the physical currency in circulation — notes and coins — was divided on the basis of the estimated population ratio (80:20) and the territorial distribution of currency chests. India received the larger share of the note issue, reflecting its larger economy, while Pakistan received the remainder, supplemented by a special issue of "Pakistan" overprinted notes printed at the Nasik press. Second, the gold and foreign exchange reserves were split in the same 80:20 proportion, with the RBI's London account being partitioned accordingly. Third, the RBI's assets and liabilities — including government securities, bills discounted, and the banking department's balance sheet — were allocated using a composite formula that weighted the revenue contribution of each province (based on 1945‑46 accounts), the location of the RBI's branches, and the outstanding credit to provincial governments.

A critical operational decision was the creation of the State Bank of Pakistan (SBP) on 1 July 1948, which assumed the central banking functions for the new dominion. Until the SBP became fully functional, the RBI's Karachi branch continued to operate under a transitional arrangement, honouring Pakistan's currency and managing its foreign exchange transactions. The RBI also transferred the staff of its Lahore, Karachi and Peshawar branches to the SBP on an "opt‑in" basis, mirroring the principle used for the armed forces. The financial settlement was formalised through the India‑Pakistan Financial Agreement of 1948, which provided for a final payment by India to Pakistan settling all monetary claims, including the share of the RBI's profit for 1947‑48.

The division had lasting consequences for monetary policy. India retained the RBI's institutional continuity, enabling a swift transition to a planned economy and the adoption of the 1949 Banking Companies Act. Pakistan, starting with a smaller reserve base and a newly created central bank, faced acute foreign exchange shortages that contributed to the 1949 devaluation of the Pakistani rupee. In exam answers, highlight the role of the Finance Sub‑Committee, the 80:20 population‑revenue formula, the transitional operation of the RBI's Karachi branch, the establishment of the State Bank of Pakistan, and the 1948 Financial Agreement as the legal closure of the asset division.

Technique: Comparative Asset‑Division Formula
A question asks: "Compare the principles used for dividing the railway network and the Reserve Bank's reserves in 1947. Why did the same 80:20 ratio appear in both cases, and what additional factors modified its application?"
Both divisions used the 80:20 population‑revenue ratio as a baseline because it reflected the broad demographic and economic weight of the two dominions. For railways, the formula was modified by route‑kilometre share and the physical location of workshops, which shifted some assets toward Pakistan (e.g., the Mughalpura workshop). For the RBI, the formula was modified by the location of currency chests, branch offices and the outstanding credit portfolio of provincial governments, which produced a slightly different effective split (India received the larger share of notes). The answer should note that the ratio was a political compromise, while the technical sub‑criteria ensured operational viability of each system.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for "the mechanism of asset division", structure the answer in three layers: (1) the high‑level political principle (population/revenue ratio), (2) the technical sub‑criteria specific to the asset class (route‑kilometres for railways, branch location for RBI), and (3) the transitional institutional arrangement (joint management, opt‑in staff transfer). This three‑layer template works for railways, RBI, armed forces, and civil service alike.

The Formation of the Indian Foreign Service and Diplomatic Continuity after Partition

The creation of a professional diplomatic corps was a prerequisite for the newly independent dominions to conduct sovereign foreign relations. The Indian Foreign Service (IFS) was formally constituted in October 1946 by a resolution of the Government of India, drawing on the pre‑war Indian Political Service and the Foreign Department of the Viceroy's Executive Council. The first batch of officers — including K. P. S. Menon, Subimal Dutt and T. N. Kaul — entered service in 1947 after a competitive examination conducted by the Federal Public Service Commission. The IFS inherited the network of diplomatic missions and consular posts that British India maintained in London, Washington, Moscow, Cairo, Tokyo and several Commonwealth capitals.

Partition required a rapid bifurcation of this diplomatic estate. The Partition Council's External Affairs Sub‑Committee, chaired by Sir Girja Shankar Bajpai (the last Secretary of the External Affairs Department), allocated missions on the basis of geographic relevance and the domicile of the serving officers. India retained the high commissions in London, Ottawa, Canberra and the embassies in Washington, Moscow, Paris and Cairo. Pakistan received the missions in Tehran, Kabul, Baghdad and the consular posts in the Persian Gulf. Officers were given the option to opt for either dominion; the majority chose India, reflecting the larger establishment and the concentration of senior officers in Delhi. Those who opted for Pakistan formed the nucleus of the Pakistan Foreign Service, formally established in 1948.

The transition was governed by the Indian Independence (External Affairs) Order 1947, which transferred all treaty obligations, diplomatic privileges and consular archives to the respective dominions. A joint committee supervised the physical handover of chancery buildings, cipher equipment and the "Blue Book" series of bilateral treaties. Notably, the 1947 India‑Pakistan Agreement on the Exchange of Diplomatic Archives ensured that each side retained copies of treaties relevant to its territorial jurisdiction. The IFS also assumed responsibility for the conduct of the 1947‑48 Kashmir negotiations at the United Nations, with the first Indian delegation led by Sir Gopalaswami Ayyangar and later by V. K. Krishna Menon.

Institutional continuity was reinforced by the retention of the Foreign Service Training Institute (later the Foreign Service Institute) in New Delhi, which continued the curriculum developed under the British. The first IFS batch set the precedent of a merit‑based, non‑political diplomatic corps, a feature that distinguished India's foreign policy apparatus from many post‑colonial states. For examination purposes, stress the 1946 resolution creating the IFS, the role of the External Affairs Sub‑Committee in mission allocation, the opt‑in principle for officers, the 1947 Order transferring treaty obligations, and the early test of the service in the UN Kashmir debates.

The Constituent Assembly's Advisory Committee on Minorities and the Drafting of Special Provisions

Among the numerous committees of the Constituent Assembly, the Advisory Committee on Minorities played a decisive role in shaping the constitutional safeguards for religious, linguistic and cultural minorities. Constituted in January 1947 under the chairmanship of Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, the committee comprised members representing the major communities — Muslims, Sikhs, Christians, Parsis, Anglo‑Indians and scheduled castes — as well as constitutional experts such as B. N. Rau and K. M. Munshi. Its mandate was to examine the demand for separate electorates, reservation in legislatures, and cultural‑educational rights, and to recommend provisions that could be incorporated into the draft Constitution.

The committee's deliberations were informed by the 1947‑48 communal violence, the experience of the 1935 Act's separate electorates, and the submissions of minority organisations — notably the All‑India Muslim League's demand for weightage, the Sikh demand for a separate state, and the Christian demand for educational autonomy. After extensive hearings, the committee rejected the continuation of separate electorates, arguing that they entrenched communal divisions and were incompatible with the principle of joint electorates adopted in the 1946 Cabinet Mission Plan. Instead, it recommended a system of proportional reservation for Muslims and other minorities in the central and provincial legislatures for a transitional period of ten years, subject to review.

On cultural and educational rights, the committee drafted Article 29 (protection of language, script and culture) and Article 30 (right of minorities to establish and administer educational institutions). These articles were modelled on the 1935 Act's Section 131 but broadened to cover all minorities, not only religious ones. The committee also proposed a National Commission for Minorities, a recommendation that was not accepted in the final Constitution but later realised through the 1992 National Commission for Minorities Act. The committee's report, submitted in August 1947, was debated in the Assembly's plenary sessions and largely incorporated into Part III (Fundamental Rights) and Part XVI (Special Provisions Relating to Certain Classes).

The legacy of the Advisory Committee is evident in the enduring tension between individual rights and group rights in Indian constitutional jurisprudence. The Supreme Court's interpretation of Article 30 in the St. Stephen's College v. University of Delhi (1992) and T.M.A. Pai Foundation v. State of Karnataka (2002) cases traces directly to the committee's framing of "minority" as a numerically defined group with the right to institutional autonomy. For high‑scoring answers, cite the committee's composition, its rejection of separate electorates, the ten‑year reservation proposal, the drafting of Articles 29 and 30, and the subsequent judicial evolution of minority rights.

The Legal Basis of the 1948‑49 Kashmir Conflict: Instrument of Accession and UN Mediation

The first India‑Pakistan war over the princely state of Jammu and Kashmir rests on a specific legal instrument — the Instrument of Accession signed by Maharaja Hari Singh on 26 October 1947 — and the subsequent involvement of the United Nations Security Council. The Instrument, executed under the Government of India Act 1935's Section 6, declared the Maharaja's accession to the Dominion of India in respect of defence, external affairs and communications, while preserving the state's internal autonomy. The document was accepted by the Governor‑General of India, Lord Mountbatten, on 27 October 1947, conditional on a future plebiscite to ascertain the will of the people.

India's immediate military intervention, codenamed Operation Jak, was justified on the basis of the Accession and the request for assistance from the Maharaja's government. Pakistan contested the legality, arguing that the Maharaja's authority had collapsed after the tribal invasion from the North‑West Frontier Province and that the Accession was signed under duress. The dispute was referred to the UN Security Council by India on 1 January 1948 under Article 35 of the UN Charter. The Council adopted Resolution 39 (1948), establishing the United Nations Commission for India and Pakistan (UNCIP), followed by Resolution 47 (21 April 1948), which called for a cease‑fire, the withdrawal of Pakistani regular forces and tribesmen, a reduction of Indian forces to minimum levels, and a plebiscite under UN auspices.

The cease‑fire line, delineated by the Karachi Agreement of 27 July 1949 between the military representatives of both dominions, became the de facto border (later termed the Line of Control after the 1972 Simla Agreement). The UNCIP's mediation efforts stalled over the sequencing of withdrawal and the composition of the plebiscite administration. India insisted on the prior withdrawal of Pakistani forces; Pakistan demanded simultaneous withdrawal and a UN‑administered plebiscite. The deadlock persisted, and the UN Military Observer Group in India and Pakistan (UNMOGIP) continues to monitor the cease‑fire line to this day.

From a constitutional perspective, the Instrument of Accession was ratified by the Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir in 1954, and the state's special status was enshrined in Article 370 of the Indian Constitution (abrogated in 2019). The UN resolutions remain a reference point in diplomatic discourse, though India maintains that the Simla Agreement (1972) supersedes them by committing both sides to bilateral resolution. For examination answers, emphasise the legal chain: Instrument of Accession → Governor‑General's acceptance → UN Security Council referral → Resolutions 39 and 47 → Karachi cease‑fire line → subsequent bilateral agreements. Highlight the conditional nature of the Accession, the role of UNCIP, and the enduring impact of the plebiscite promise on Indo‑Pakistani relations.

  • Confusing the 1947 Railway Sub‑Committee's territorial principle with the 1948 Financial Agreement's revenue‑based formula; they operated on different asset classes and produced distinct effective splits.
  • Assuming the State Bank of Pakistan began operations on 15 August 1947; it was formally inaugurated on 1 July 1948, with the RBI's Karachi branch functioning as an interim central bank.
  • Attributing the creation of the Indian Foreign Service to the 1947 Independence Act; the IFS was constituted by a 1946 Government of India resolution, before the transfer of power.
  • Stating that the Advisory Committee on Minorities recommended permanent separate electorates; it explicitly rejected them and proposed a ten‑year reservation scheme.
  • Treating the 1948 Karachi Agreement as a formal international treaty; it was a military cease‑fire arrangement without full diplomatic ratification, later supplemented by the Simla Agreement.
  • Overlooking the conditional plebiscite clause in the Instrument of Accession and presenting the Accession as unconditional; the Governor‑General's acceptance letter explicitly linked finality to a popular vote.
  • Mixing the UNCIP's 1948 resolutions with the later UNMOGIP mandate; UNCIP was a mediation body, while UNMOGIP is a monitoring mission established after the cease‑fire.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Postal and Telegraph Services

The Partition Council, constituted under the Indian Independence Act 1947, mandated the division of the Posts and Telegraphs Department on a territorial basis. The Director General of Posts and Telegraphs oversaw the bifurcation of a substantial network of post offices, telegraph offices and telegraph lines. Assets were allocated according to the 1947 Postal and Telegraph (Partition) Order, which prescribed that offices situated in the new dominion of Pakistan would transfer to the newly created Pakistan Post and Telegraph Department, while those in India remained under the Indian Posts and Telegraphs Department. Staff options were offered: employees could opt for service in either dominion, with pension liabilities apportioned on a pro‑rata basis of service years. The Order also provided for the continuation of existing telegraph links between major centres until new infrastructure could be established, ensuring uninterrupted communication during the transition. The division of postal savings bank balances followed the same territorial principle, with the Reserve Bank of India acting as the clearing house for the transfer of deposits. By August 1947, the Indian side retained the majority of post offices and the telegraph network was split into two independent systems, each with its own director‑general. The reorganisation was completed within months, and the first Pakistan postage stamps, inscribed “Pakistan Postage”, were issued in 1948, marking the operational independence of the new postal administration.

The Role of the All‑India Muslim League’s Provincial Branches in Mobilising Support for Partition (1937‑1946)

After the 1937 provincial elections, the Muslim League’s provincial organisations became the primary vehicles for translating the Lahore Resolution into mass support. In Punjab, the League negotiated the Sikandar‑Jinnah Pact (1937), which gave the League a foothold in the Unionist‑dominated ministry while preserving the Unionist coalition. In Bengal, the League’s alliance with the Krishak Praja Party led to the formation of a coalition ministry under A. K. Fazlul Huq (1937‑1941), providing the League with administrative patronage and a platform to propagate the two‑nation theory. Sind’s League branch leveraged the province’s separate electorate system to secure a League ministry in 1943, which then facilitated a major provincial conference that endorsed the demand for a sovereign Muslim state. The North‑West Frontier Province presented a distinct challenge; the Congress‑aligned Khudai Khidmatgar movement dominated, but the League’s provincial branch, revitalised after the 1945‑46 elections, captured a significant share of seats, enabling the League to claim representation of the Pashtun electorate. The 1945‑46 general elections proved decisive: the League won the overwhelming majority of Muslim seats across provinces, a mandate it cited as the “plebiscite” for Pakistan. Provincial branches organised “Direct Action” committees, coordinated mass mobilisation campaigns, and managed the logistics of the 1946‑47 mobilisation that culminated in the Partition Council’s acceptance of the Mountbatten Plan.

Examiner's technique
A question asks: “Explain how the Muslim League’s provincial branches converted the Lahore Resolution into electoral success in the 1945‑46 elections.”
The answer must link three mechanisms: (1) alliance‑building with regional parties (Unionists in Punjab, Krishak Praja in Bengal) to gain ministerial control; (2) use of separate electorates to concentrate Muslim votes, turning provincial ministries into patronage networks that funded League campaigns; (3) the 1945‑46 election campaign’s focus on the “Pakistan” slogan, coordinated by provincial “Direct Action” committees that mobilised volunteers, controlled local press, and organised mass meetings. Cite the seat tally — the League’s capture of the vast majority of Muslim seats — as quantitative proof of the conversion.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for “provincial dynamics”, structure the answer by province (Punjab, Bengal, Sind, NWFP) and for each give: (a) pre‑1937 political landscape, (b) League’s alliance or opposition, (c) 1945‑46 seat outcome. This tabular mental map prevents omission of any province and scores full marks for comprehensiveness.

The 1942‑43 War Economy, Industrial Labour and the Growth of Trade Union Militancy

The Defence of India Act 1939 and the subsequent Defence of India Rules empowered the colonial state to regulate wages, hours and industrial disputes in war‑essential sectors. The 1942‑43 period saw a sharp rise in the cost of living index, while real wages in textile mills of Bombay and Ahmedabad fell significantly. The All‑India Trade Union Congress (AITUC), dominated by the Communist Party of India after 1942, organised a major general strike in Bombay in July 1942 involving a large number of workers, demanding a substantial wage increase and the release of political prisoners. The colonial response invoked the Defence of India Rules, leading to the arrest of numerous trade‑union leaders and the imposition of the “Essential Services Maintenance Ordinance” (1943) which criminalised strikes in designated industries. Despite repression, the 1943 Bengal famine intensified rural‑urban migration, swelling the industrial labour pool in Calcutta’s jute mills. The Communist‑led Bengal Provincial Trade Union Federation coordinated a major jute workers’ strike in 1944, securing a wage hike after a prolonged stoppage. By 1945, the AITUC claimed a membership in the millions, and the 1945‑46 “Post‑War Strike Wave” — involving hundreds of strikes and millions of workers — forced the interim government to appoint the “Industrial Truce Committee” (1946) which recommended the Industrial Disputes Act 1947, laying the statutory framework for post‑independence labour relations.

The 1946‑47 Interim Government’s Fiscal Policy: Taxation, Currency Stabilisation and the Pre‑Independence Budget

The Interim Government, sworn in on 2 September 1946, inherited a large fiscal deficit driven by war expenditure and the 1943 famine relief outlay. Finance Member Sir John Mathai presented the 1947‑48 budget in February 1947, proposing increased income‑tax rates for higher incomes, a new “Excess Profits Tax” on war‑time industrial profits, and a surcharge on customs duties to fund the Partition Council’s administrative costs. Currency stabilisation was pursued through the “Rupee‑Sterling Parity Agreement” of 1946, which fixed the rupee at 1 shilling 6 pence, and the Reserve Bank of India’s “Currency Reserve Ratio” was raised to curb inflationary pressure. The budget also allocated substantial funds for the “Refugee Rehabilitation Fund” and the “Princely States Integration Fund”. The Partition Council’s Financial Committee oversaw the division of the Reserve Bank’s assets: gold and sterling reserves were split in a ratio reflecting the territorial distribution of currency circulation. The interim government’s fiscal measures, though constrained by the impending transfer of power, established the revenue base for the first Union Budget of independent India presented by R. K. Shanmukham Chetty in November 1947.

The 1947‑48 Integration of the North‑East Frontier Agency and the Tribal Areas: Administrative Mechanics and Constitutional Provisions

The North‑East Frontier Agency (NEFA) and the adjoining tribal tracts were administered under the Government of India Act 1935’s “Excluded Areas” and “Partially Excluded Areas” provisions. After 15 August 1947, the Governor of Assam exercised direct executive authority over NEFA through the “North‑East Frontier (Administration) Regulation, 1948”, which replaced the earlier “Frontier Tracts Regulation”. The Regulation vested the Governor with legislative powers for the area, subject to the assent of the Governor‑General. Simultaneously, the Ministry of States, headed by Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, negotiated Instruments of Accession with the princely states of Manipur (the process began with a 1947 resolution and culminated in accession in 1949) and Tripura (acceded in August 1947). The tribal areas of the Naga Hills, Lushai Hills and the Abor Hills were brought under the “Sixth Schedule” of the Constitution (adopted 26 January 1950) which provided for Autonomous District Councils with legislative, executive and judicial powers. The 1948 “Naga National Council” memorandum demanded a separate sovereign Naga state; the Government of India responded with the “Nine‑Point Agreement” (June 1948) granting limited autonomy and a promise of future constitutional safeguards. The administrative integration was completed by 1950 when NEFA was placed under the direct control of the Ministry of External Affairs, and the first Autonomous District Councils were constituted in the early 1950s, marking the transition from colonial frontier administration to constitutional tribal self‑governance.

  • Confusing the 1947 Postal and Telegraph (Partition) Order with the 1948 Financial Agreement; they governed different asset classes and produced distinct split ratios.
  • Attributing the 1945‑46 Muslim League electoral sweep solely to the Lahore Resolution, ignoring the role of provincial alliances and separate electorates.
  • Treating the 1942‑43 General Strike as a purely Communist action, overlooking the participation of Congress‑aligned unions and the impact of the Defence of India Rules.
  • Assuming the Interim Government’s 1947‑48 budget was the first Union Budget of independent India; the first Union Budget was presented in November 1947 by R. K. Shanmukham Chetty.
  • Equating the 1948 North‑East Frontier (Administration) Regulation with the Sixth Schedule; the Regulation was an executive instrument, while the Sixth Schedule is a constitutional provision enacted in 1950.
  • Overlooking the conditional nature of the 1948 Nine‑Point Agreement with the Naga National Council, which did not concede sovereignty but promised future autonomy.
  • Misplacing the date of the Rupee‑Sterling Parity Agreement; it was concluded in 1946, not 1947, and fixed the rupee at 1 shilling 6 pence.

The advanced sections above provide the mechanistic detail and comparative nuance required for high‑scoring answers on the final phase of the freedom struggle and the immediate post‑partition administrative reorganisation.

The Congress Constructive Programme: Khadi, Village Industries, and Social Reform (1920‑1947)

The Constructive Programme was adopted at the Nagpur session of the Indian National Congress in December 1920 as the non‑violent counterpart to mass civil disobedience. Gandhi argued that political independence would be meaningless without economic self‑reliance and social regeneration. The programme centred on three pillars: the propagation of khadi (hand‑spun, hand‑woven cloth), the revival of village industries, and the eradication of untouchability through the Harijan Sevak Sangh.

Khadi became the most visible symbol. The All‑India Spinners Association (AISA) was formed in 1925 under Gandhi's guidance. By the late 1930s the AISA reported production in the tens of millions of yards annually, employing hundreds of thousands of spinners and weavers, predominantly women in rural households. British tariff measures on imported mill cloth in the early 1930s inadvertently stimulated domestic demand for khadi.

Village industries received institutional support through the All‑India Village Industries Association (AIVIA), set up in 1935 with support from industrialists including G. D. Birla. AIVIA promoted oil‑pressing, pottery, leatherwork, and bee‑keeping, providing credit through the newly created Village Industries Fund. The 1937‑38 Congress ministries in provinces such as United Provinces and Bihar allocated budgetary grants for village‑industry training centres, linking the programme to provincial autonomy.

Social reform was pursued through the Harijan Sevak Sangh, founded in 1932 after Gandhi’s fast against the Communal Award. The Sangh ran schools, hostels, and temple‑entry campaigns, and by 1946 reported a network of over a thousand workers across hundreds of districts. Simultaneously, Gandhi’s “Nai Talim” (Basic Education) scheme, launched at the 1937 Wardha conference, integrated productive manual labour with literacy, aiming to make education self‑supporting and rooted in village life.

Despite its moral appeal, the Constructive Programme faced structural limits. Khadi never displaced mill cloth in urban markets; the wartime economy diverted raw cotton to British textile mills. Village industries remained marginal because of inadequate credit, lack of marketing channels, and competition from cheaper factory goods. The Harijan Sevak Sangh’s temple‑entry campaigns met with violent resistance in some princely states, including Travancore and Mysore. Nevertheless, the programme forged a cadre of constructive workers who later formed the backbone of the Quit India underground and the post‑1947 community‑development blocks.

The Quit India Movement’s Underground Communication Networks and Parallel Governance (1942‑1944)

Following the arrest of the entire Congress Working Committee on 9 August 1942, the movement survived through a decentralized web of covert communication and parallel administrations. The most celebrated node was the “Congress Radio”, a clandestine short‑wave station operated from Bombay by Usha Mehta, Vithalbhai Jhaveri, and a team of student volunteers. Broadcasting on a short‑wave frequency from August 1942 until its detection in late 1943, it transmitted Gandhi’s “Do or Die” message, news of British repression, and guidance for civil resistance.

Courier networks linked provincial hubs. In the United Provinces, groups such as the “Azad Dasta” used railway employees to carry coded letters hidden in luggage. In Bengal, district‑level coordinators communicated through pre‑arranged symbols in newspaper advertisements. The All‑India Students Federation (AISF) and the All‑India Women’s Conference (AIWC) supplied safe houses and cyclostyle printing presses; the latter produced the “Quit India Bulletin” in Hindi, Marathi, and Tamil, with a peak circulation of several thousand copies per issue.

Parallel governments emerged where British authority collapsed. The most durable was the “Prati Sarkar” in Satara district (Maharashtra), led by Nana Patil (known as Krantisinh Nana Patil), which collected taxes, ran arbitration courts, and maintained a volunteer militia from mid‑1943 to mid‑1944. In Tamluk (Midnapore, Bengal), the “Tamralipta Jatiya Sarkar” under Satish Chandra Samanta administered relief during the 1943 cyclone and organized grain distribution. Ballia (United Provinces) saw a short‑lived “Azad Government” headed by Chittu Pandey, which declared independence in August 1942 before being suppressed within days.

British counter‑measures included the Defence of India Rules (1939) amended in 1942 to permit preventive detention without trial, the Press (Emergency Powers) Act (1931) to seize printing presses, and the deployment of the “Special Branch” to infiltrate courier rings. By late 1944, most radio operators had been arrested, and the parallel administrations were dismantled through combined military‑police operations. Yet the experience of self‑governance in Satara and Tamluk provided a template for post‑war “Gram Panchayat” experiments and influenced the 1946‑47 Constituent Assembly debates on village autonomy.

Technique: Mapping Covert Networks
Question: Explain how the Congress Radio and the Prati Sarkar in Satara illustrate the dual strategy of communication and governance during the Quit India Movement.
The Congress Radio functioned as an information‑distribution hub: it broadcast Gandhi’s directives, countered British propaganda, and coordinated resistance timetables across linguistic regions. Its operators used a mobile transmitter, changing locations nightly, and relied on a trusted courier chain of students and railway workers to deliver tapes and receive feedback. The Prati Sarkar, by contrast, translated that communication into administrative action: it established a parallel revenue system (collecting a voluntary “national tax”), set up people’s courts to resolve disputes, and organized a volunteer defence force that protected villages from police reprisals. Together they demonstrate that the underground was not merely a propaganda tool but a functional substitute for colonial administration, sustaining morale and providing tangible services that legitimized the nationalist claim to sovereignty.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “underground communication” or “parallel government”, structure the answer in two columns: (1) Communication – name the radio, courier, press, and coding methods; (2) Governance – name the parallel administration, its functions (tax, justice, defence), and its geographic spread. Cite at least two concrete examples (Congress Radio, Prati Sarkar, Tamluk Jatiya Sarkar) and link them to the British legal response (Defence of India Rules, Press Emergency Powers Act). This two‑column framework ensures coverage of both dimensions and scores full marks for “mechanism” and “impact”.

The Interim Government’s Management of Communal Violence in Punjab and Bengal (1946‑1947)

The Interim Government, sworn in on 2 September 1946 under the viceroyalty of Lord Wavell, inherited a volatile communal landscape. The Home portfolio was held by Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, who directed the Indian Police and the British Indian Army through the Defence Council. The first major test came with the Calcutta “Direct Action Day” on 16 August 1946, where Muslim League‑called hartals escalated into four days of rioting, leaving several thousand dead. The Interim Government responded by deploying British Indian Army divisions and imposing a curfew under Section 144 of the Criminal Procedure Code, but the police force, heavily communalized, often stood by.

In October 1946, the Noakhali and Tipperah districts of East Bengal witnessed targeted attacks on Hindu minorities. Gandhi undertook an extensive peace mission in Noakhali from November 1946; the Interim Government facilitated his mission by providing security and logistical support. Simultaneously, the Bihar riots (late October 1946) saw Hindu mobs retaliate against Muslims; Patel ordered the arrest of local Congress leaders accused of incitement, a move that drew criticism from within the party.

The Punjab situation deteriorated after the March 1947 Rawalpindi massacres. The Interim Government, now under Mountbatten’s viceroyalty (from 24 March), created the Punjab Boundary Force (PBF) in July 1947 under Major General Rees, comprising tens of thousands of troops drawn from the British Indian Army. The PBF’s mandate was to maintain law and order during the partition of the province. However, the force was hamstrung by communal composition — Sikh, Hindu, and Muslim units often refused to fire on co‑religionists — and by the absence of a clear political directive after the 3 June 1947 Partition Plan. The Interim Government’s final act was the evacuation of non‑Muslim officials from West Punjab and Muslim officials from East Punjab, coordinated through the Partition Council’s Relief and Rehabilitation Committee.

Overall, the Interim Government’s management was reactive, constrained by the dual authority of the Viceroy’s Executive Council and the British Commander‑in‑Chief. Its reliance on a communalized police, the limited size of the PBF, and the absence of a unified political command meant that violence continued unabated until the Radcliffe Award (17 August 1947) and the subsequent mass migrations. The experience underscored the necessity of a neutral, centrally controlled paramilitary force — a lesson that informed the strengthening of the Central Reserve Police Force after independence.

The Communist Party of India’s Electoral Strategy in the 1945‑46 Provincial Elections

The Communist Party of India (CPI) entered the 1945‑46 provincial elections after a dramatic policy reversal. Following the Nazi invasion of the Soviet Union in June 1941, the CPI shifted from an anti‑war “imperialist war” stance to “People’s War” support for the Allied cause, aligning itself with the British war effort. This enabled the party to operate legally, expand its trade‑union base through the All‑India Trade Union Congress (AITUC), and build peasant fronts such as the All‑India Kisan Sabha (AIKS).

In the elections, the CPI contested over a hundred seats across eleven provinces, fielding candidates primarily in industrial and agrarian constituencies: Bombay (textile mills), Bengal (jute mills and peasant belts), Madras (plantations), and the Punjab (canal colonies). The party secured eight seats — three in Bengal (including a seat won by Jyoti Basu), two in Madras, and one each in Bombay, Punjab, and the Central Provinces. Its vote share was modest (approximately 2‑3 % of the total electorate), but it captured the imagination of industrial workers and tenant farmers.

The CPI’s campaign emphasized “National Unity against Fascism”, “Land to the Tiller”, and “Workers’ Control of Industry”. It forged tactical electoral understandings with the Congress in Bengal and Madras, where Congress leaders accommodated communist candidates to avoid splitting the anti‑League vote. In the Punjab, the CPI opposed the Muslim League’s demand for Pakistan, arguing for a united, secular Punjab — a position that alienated both the League and the Akali Dal.

Post‑election, the CPI’s influence peaked during the 1946‑47 Tebhaga movement in Bengal and the 1947‑48 Telangana armed struggle. However, the party’s stance on the Mountbatten Plan and its wartime “People’s War” line led to its marginalization within the nationalist spectrum. In March 1948, the Government of India banned the CPI under the Criminal Law Amendment Act, citing the Telangana uprising as a threat to internal security. The ban forced the party underground until its conditional legalization in 1951.

  • Confusing the Congress Constructive Programme’s khadi output with the 1930‑31 Civil Disobedience boycott figures; they are distinct economic activities.
  • Attributing the Congress Radio’s broadcasts to the Azad Hind Radio of Subhas Chandra Bose; the former was an internal Congress operation, the latter an external INA broadcast.
  • Assuming the Punjab Boundary Force was a neutral UN‑style peacekeeping force; it was a British Indian Army formation with communalized units.
  • Overstating the Communist Party of India’s seat tally in 1945‑46; it won only eight provincial seats, not a significant legislative bloc.
  • Equating the Interim Government’s Home Member (Patel) with the post‑independence Home Minister; the former operated under the Viceroy’s Executive Council, the latter under the Prime Minister.
  • Neglecting the role of the Defence of India Rules (1939) in legitimizing preventive detention of underground workers during Quit India.
  • Treating the 1946‑47 communal violence as solely a spontaneous outbreak, ignoring the Interim Government’s deliberate deployment of the Punjab Boundary Force and its political constraints.

The advanced sections above integrate organisational mechanics, covert operations, administrative responses, and electoral tactics to give a comprehensive view of the final phase of the freedom struggle.

The 1946 Royal Indian Navy Mutiny: Causes, Course, and Consequences

The Royal Indian Navy (RIN) mutiny erupted on 18 February 1946 at the shore establishment HMIS Talwar in Bombay. The immediate trigger was a combination of long‑standing grievances: discriminatory pay scales between Indian and British ratings, poor quality food, racial insults from British officers, and the denial of shore leave. The political climate amplified these grievances — the recent INA trials (November 1945–January 1946) had turned the Indian soldier into a nationalist symbol, and the Congress, Muslim League, and Communist Party all issued statements of solidarity with the ratings.

Within 48 hours the strike spread to 78 ships and 20 shore establishments across Bombay, Karachi, Madras, Calcutta, and the Andaman Islands, involving roughly 20,000 ratings. The mutineers hoisted the tricolour alongside the Muslim League flag, formed a Naval Central Strike Committee, and demanded the release of INA prisoners, equal pay, and the withdrawal of British troops from India. The British responded with a naval blockade, deployment of Royal Marines, and low‑flying aircraft over the harbour as intimidation. By 23 February the uprising was crushed; official figures record eight ratings killed and 33 wounded, though nationalist sources claim higher casualties.

Politically, the Congress leadership — Gandhi, Nehru, and Patel — urged the ratings to surrender, fearing that an uncontrolled armed revolt would give the British a pretext for harsher repression and jeopardise the forthcoming Cabinet Mission. The Muslim League and the Communist Party, however, praised the mutineers as patriots. The episode demonstrated that the loyalty of the Indian armed forces could no longer be taken for granted. It underscored for the Attlee government that the “Indian Army” was a political actor, accelerating the timetable for transfer of power and influencing the Cabinet Mission’s emphasis on a negotiated settlement.

The Cripps Mission of 1942: Proposals, Congress Rejection, and Strategic Implications

In March 1942 Sir Stafford Cripps, a senior Labour minister, arrived in Delhi bearing a British government offer designed to secure full Indian cooperation in the war effort. The proposal promised dominion status after the war, a constituent assembly elected by provincial legislatures to frame a new constitution, and the right of any province to opt out of the future union — a clause aimed at accommodating the Muslim League’s demand for Pakistan. The British also pledged that the Viceroy’s Executive Council would be expanded to include Indian leaders, though defence and foreign affairs would remain under British control for the duration of the war.

The Congress Working Committee, meeting at Wardha, rejected the offer on 11 April 1942. The principal objections were: (1) no immediate transfer of power, leaving the British in effective control during the war; (2) the opt‑out clause legitimised partition before a constituent assembly could deliberate; (3) the defence portfolio remained British, denying Indians any say in the war that directly affected them. The Muslim League also rejected the plan, insisting on a clear commitment to Pakistan. The failure of the mission convinced Gandhi that the British would not concede independence voluntarily, prompting the launch of the Quit India Movement on 8 August 1942.

Strategically, the Cripps Mission exposed the limits of British wartime diplomacy. It showed that the British were willing to negotiate only on terms that preserved imperial interests, while Indian nationalist opinion had shifted irrevocably toward complete sovereignty. The mission’s collapse also deepened the Congress–League divide, because the opt‑out provision gave the League a constitutional lever it would later exploit during the Cabinet Mission negotiations.

Examiner's technique: Comparative evaluation of British offers
Compare the Cripps Offer (1942) with the Cabinet Mission Plan (1946) on the parameters of (a) immediate power transfer, (b) provincial grouping, and (c) defence control. Which offer was more acceptable to the Congress and why?
The Cripps Offer deferred power transfer until after the war, retained British control of defence, and allowed provinces to opt out individually. The Cabinet Mission Plan proposed an immediate interim government, grouped provinces into three sections (A, B, C) with a weak centre, and kept defence under the Viceroy until a new constitution. Congress found the Cabinet Mission relatively more acceptable because it offered an immediate executive role and a structured grouping that preserved unity, whereas Cripps’ individual opt‑out clause was seen as a recipe for fragmentation. The Congress therefore engaged with the Cabinet Mission while rejecting Cripps.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks you to “compare” two British proposals, tabulate the three key dimensions (power transfer, grouping, defence) in a quick mental table. The examiner rewards precise differentiation — note the shift from individual opt‑out (Cripps) to compulsory grouping (Cabinet Mission) and the move from deferred to immediate interim government.

Women's Participation in Gandhian Mass Movements (1919‑1947): Organisation, Agency, and Historiography

Women entered the nationalist arena in unprecedented numbers during the Non‑Cooperation Movement (1920‑22), responding to Gandhi’s call for boycott of foreign cloth and picketing of liquor shops. The All India Women’s Conference (AIWC), founded in 1927, provided an organisational platform that linked social reform — education, age of consent, purdah — with political mobilisation. During the 1930 Salt Satyagraha, women such as Sarojini Naidu, Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay, and Mithuben Petit led processions to the sea, manufactured salt, and courted arrest; thousands of women were imprisoned in that campaign alone.

The Quit India Movement (1942) saw women assume leadership roles when male leaders were incarcerated. Aruna Asaf Ali hoisted the Congress flag at Gowalia Tank Maidan, Usha Mehta ran the clandestine Congress Radio from Bombay, and Sucheta Kriplani organised underground networks in the United Provinces. In Bengal, Matangini Hazra led a procession of women that was fired upon at Tamluk, becoming a martyr. The Communist Party’s women’s wing, the Mahila Atma Raksha Samiti, mobilised peasant women in the Tebhaga struggle (1946‑47), linking class and gender oppression.

Historiographically, early nationalist narratives portrayed women as passive supporters of male leaders. Since the 1970s, scholars such as Geraldine Forbes, Tanika Sarkar, and Charu Gupta have demonstrated that women negotiated patriarchal constraints within the Congress, the Muslim League, and left‑wing organisations, carving out autonomous political identities. Their participation reshaped the discourse on citizenship, culminating in the Constituent Assembly’s debates on gender‑neutral franchise and the Hindu Code Bills.

The 1935 Government of India Act and Provincial Autonomy: Congress Ministries (1937‑39) and Their Resignation

The Government of India Act 1935 introduced provincial autonomy, abolishing dyarchy in the provinces and establishing elected legislatures with control over all provincial subjects except law and order, which remained under the governor’s special responsibilities. The 1937 elections, held under a limited franchise (about 14 % of adults), returned Congress majorities in seven of eleven provinces: Madras, Bombay, Central Provinces, United Provinces, Bihar, Orissa, and the North‑West Frontier Province.

Congress ministries enacted a range of reforms: tenancy legislation in Bihar and United Provinces granting occupancy rights to tenants; reduction of land revenue in Madras; expansion of primary education and public health services; and the release of political prisoners. They also used the platform to promote civil liberties, lifting bans on nationalist newspapers and allowing trade‑union activity. However, the governors retained veto powers and could dismiss ministries, a constraint that became critical when the Viceroy, Lord Linlithgow, declared India a belligerent in the Second World War on 3 September 1939 without consulting Indian leaders.

In protest, the Congress Working Committee directed all provincial ministries to resign by November 1939. The resignations created a political vacuum that the Muslim League filled by observing 22 December 1939 as the “Day of Deliverance”, claiming that Congress rule had been oppressive to Muslims. The episode hardened communal polarisation, gave the League a propaganda victory, and demonstrated the fragility of provincial autonomy under a colonial centre that could unilaterally commit India to war.

The Rowlatt Act and the 1919 Satyagraha: Legal Architecture, Repression, and Political Fallout

The Rowlatt Act, formally the Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act 1919, extended wartime emergency powers into peacetime. It authorised the government to detain suspects without trial for up to two years, conduct in‑camera trials, and restrict press freedom. The legislation was based on the recommendations of the Rowlatt Committee (1918), which warned of a “revolutionary conspiracy” linked to the Ghadar movement and the 1915 Singapore mutiny.

Gandhi, then emerging as a national leader, called for a nationwide hartal on 6 April 1919 — the first all‑India mass civil‑disobedience campaign. The response was massive: shops closed, workers struck, and public meetings defied the ban. In Punjab, Lieutenant‑Governor Sir Michael O’Dwyer imposed martial law. On 13 April 1919, Brigadier‑General Reginald Dyer ordered troops to fire on an unarmed gathering at Jallianwala Bagh, Amritsar, killing at least 379 (official figure) and wounding over a thousand. The massacre triggered widespread outrage, prompting Rabindranath Tagore to renounce his knighthood and Gandhi to suspend the satyagraha on 18 April.

The political fallout was profound. The Hunter Committee (1920) condemned Dyer’s action but upheld the Rowlatt Act’s legality, exposing the limits of British justice. The Indian National Congress, at its Amritsar session (December 1919), adopted the goal of “Swaraj” through “peaceful and legitimate means”, marking a shift from petitioning to mass mobilisation. The Montagu‑Chelmsford Reforms (Government of India Act 1919) were announced simultaneously, introducing dyarchy in the provinces — a concession that failed to satisfy nationalist aspirations and set the stage for the Non‑Cooperation Movement (1920‑22).

  • Confusing the 1946 RIN mutiny with the 1942 Quit India underground; the former was a naval strike, the latter a civilian uprising.
  • Attributing the Cripps Offer’s opt‑out clause to the Cabinet Mission; the Mission used compulsory grouping, not individual provincial secession.
  • Overstating women’s formal representation in Congress ministries; no woman held a ministerial portfolio in 1937‑39.
