  • Assuming the Rowlatt Act was repealed immediately after Jallianwala Bagh; it remained on the statute book until the 1922 repeal.
  • Treating the 1935 Act’s provincial autonomy as full self‑government; governors retained overriding powers and the centre kept control of defence and foreign affairs.
  • Equating the 1946 RIN mutineers’ demands with the INA’s political programme; the ratings focused on service conditions, not a formal declaration of independence.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise factual recall and contextual analysis.

The Non‑Cooperation Movement (1920‑22): Organisation, Boycott Strategies, and the Chauri Chaura Turning Point

Launch and Organisational Framework

The Non‑Cooperation Movement was formally launched at the Indian National Congress session in Nagpur in December 1920, following Gandhi’s call for “non‑violent non‑cooperation” with the colonial state. The programme combined a mass boycott of government‑run institutions — schools, colleges, law courts, legislative councils, and foreign‑made cloth — with the constructive promotion of khadi, national schools, and village industries. Provincial Congress Committees were tasked with organising local hartals, picketing of liquor shops, and the surrender of titles and honours. The movement drew in a broad social base: peasants in the United Provinces and Bihar, industrial workers in Bombay and Ahmedabad, students in Bengal, and the urban middle class across the presidencies.

Boycott Mechanics and Economic Impact

Boycott of foreign cloth was the most visible economic weapon. By mid‑1921, imports of British piece‑goods fell by an estimated 30 % in the Bombay Presidency alone, while khadi production rose from a negligible base to over 30 million yards annually. The boycott of government schools led to the establishment of over 200 national schools in the first year, notably in Gujarat and the Madras Presidency. Lawyers such as Motilal Nehru and C. R. Das resigned their practices, and many municipal councillors resigned, creating administrative vacuums that the British filled with nominated officials.

Repression and the Chauri Chaura Incident

The colonial response combined legal measures — the extension of the Defence of India Act, the use of the Seditious Meetings Act, and mass arrests — with police firings on demonstrators. The turning point came on 5 February 1922 at Chauri Chaura (Gorakhpur district), where a peaceful procession clashed with police, resulting in the burning of a police station and the death of 22 constables. Gandhi, interpreting the violence as a breach of the movement’s non‑violent discipline, announced the immediate suspension of Non‑Cooperation on 12 February 1922. The decision split the Congress: the “No‑Changers” (led by C. R. Das and Motilal Nehru) advocated council entry, while the “Pro‑Changers” (including Gandhi) insisted on constructive work.

Legacy for Subsequent Mass Mobilisation

Although suspended, the movement expanded the political constituency, introduced mass civil‑disobedience techniques (hartal, picketing, boycott), and forged a network of volunteers that underpinned the 1930‑34 Civil Disobedience Movement. It also demonstrated the limits of a purely moral strategy when confronted with state violence, a lesson that shaped Gandhi’s later emphasis on “strategic non‑violence” combined with negotiated settlements.

The Civil Disobedience Movement (1930‑34): Salt Satyagraha, Regional Variations, and British Legal Response

Dandi March and the Symbolic Violation of the Salt Law

On 12 March 1930 Gandhi set out from Sabarmati Ashram with 78 followers on a 240‑mile march to the coastal village of Dandi, arriving on 6 April. By publicly evaporating seawater to produce salt, he violated the 1882 British Salt Act, which granted the state a monopoly on salt production and imposed a heavy tax. The act was chosen for its universal impact — salt was a daily necessity for every Indian — and for the ease with which ordinary citizens could replicate the defiance.

Spread and Regional Character

The salt satyagraha triggered parallel actions across the subcontinent. In Bengal, C. R. Das led a boycott of British goods and a refusal to pay the chowkidari tax. In the North‑West Frontier Province, Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan’s Khudai Khidmatgar volunteers organised non‑violent resistance despite severe repression. In Madras, the “Vedaranyam March” led by C. Rajagopalachari mirrored Dandi. In Punjab, the movement merged with the ongoing Sikh demand for control of gurdwaras, while in Bombay the mill workers staged massive strikes. Women participated in unprecedented numbers — Sarojini Naidu, Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay, and thousands of ordinary women picketed liquor shops and sold contraband salt.

British Legal Counter‑Measures

The colonial administration responded with a battery of ordinances: the Emergency Powers Ordinance (1930), the Criminal Law Amendment Ordinance (1930), and the Press (Emergency Powers) Ordinance (1931). Over 60,000 arrests were made by the end of 1930, including Gandhi, Nehru, and Patel. Special tribunals conducted summary trials, and the “Salt Act” was amended to increase penalties. The Viceroy, Lord Irwin, eventually negotiated the Gandhi‑Irwin Pact (5 March 1931), which secured the release of political prisoners and the right to make salt for personal use, but fell short of conceding dominion status.

Second Phase, Communal Award, and Poona Pact

After the failure of the Second Round Table Conference (September‑December 1931), the movement resumed in January 1932 with a renewed boycott of British goods and a refusal to pay land revenue. The British responded with the Communal Award (August 1932), granting separate electorates to depressed classes. Gandhi’s fast unto death in Yerwada Jail forced the Poona Pact (September 1932), which replaced separate electorates with reserved seats within a joint electorate. The movement was formally suspended in May 1934 after the Congress accepted the 1935 Government of India Act’s provincial autonomy provisions, marking a strategic shift toward constitutional politics.

Examiner's Technique: Comparative Cause‑Effect Mapping
Question: Compare the immediate triggers and the British legal responses of the Non‑Cooperation Movement (1920‑22) and the Civil Disobedience Movement (1930‑34). Identify two similarities and two differences.
Similarities: (1) Both movements were launched by Gandhi after a Congress session (Nagpur 1920; Lahore 1929) and employed mass boycott of colonial institutions. (2) In both cases the British used emergency ordinances and mass arrests to suppress dissent. Differences: (1) The Non‑Cooperation trigger was the Rowlatt Act and the Jallianwala Bagh massacre, whereas the Civil Disobedience trigger was the Salt Tax and the rejection of the Simon Commission. (2) The Non‑Cooperation Movement was suspended after a single violent incident (Chauri Chaura), while the Civil Disobedience Movement continued through two distinct phases, interrupted by the Gandhi‑Irwin Pact and the Communal Award, before a formal suspension in 1934.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “compare and contrast”, structure the answer in a two‑column table (mental or on paper) with headings Trigger, British Response, Outcome. Fill each column with precise facts — dates, ordinance names, key personalities — before writing the prose. This prevents vague generalisations and ensures you hit the required number of points.

The Quit India Movement (1942‑44): Impact on British War Production and International Diplomatic Pressure

Launch and Immediate British Crackdown

The All‑India Congress Committee passed the “Quit India” resolution on 8 August 1942 at Bombay’s Gowalia Tank Maidan, demanding an immediate end to British rule. Gandhi’s “Do or Die” call sparked spontaneous hartals, strikes, and attacks on railway lines, telegraph wires, and government buildings across the Gangetic plain, the Deccan, and the eastern provinces. Within 48 hours the British arrested the entire Congress Working Committee, including Gandhi, Nehru, and Patel, and imposed the Defence of India Act to legitimise summary detention.

Disruption of War‑Time Logistics

The movement’s sabotage campaign targeted the arterial infrastructure that sustained the Allied war effort in the China‑Burma‑India theatre. Railway workshops at Jamalpur and Mughalsarai were paralysed for weeks; the East Indian Railway lost an estimated 15 % of its rolling stock to arson and derailment. Telegraph lines linking Calcutta with the front in Burma were cut repeatedly, delaying intelligence and supply coordination. The British Indian Army’s recruitment drives in the Punjab and the United Provinces fell by nearly 20 % in the last quarter of 1942, as local officials reported widespread refusal to enlist.

Underground Parallel Governments

In the absence of top leadership, local Congress volunteers formed parallel administrations — notably the “Ballia Republic” (August‑September 1942), the “Tamluk National Government” in Midnapore (December 1942‑January 1943), and the “Prati Sarkar” in Satara (1943‑44). These bodies collected taxes, ran courts, and organised food distribution, demonstrating the capacity of nationalist networks to assume governance functions under repression.

International Diplomatic Repercussions

The scale of the uprising forced the British to justify their rule to Allied partners. The United States, under President Roosevelt, pressed Churchill to offer post‑war self‑government, citing the Atlantic Charter’s principle of self‑determination. Chinese leader Chiang Kai‑shek, concerned about the stability of the Burma Road, urged a political settlement. In the British Parliament, the Labour opposition highlighted the contradiction of fighting fascism abroad while denying democracy in India. These pressures contributed to the 1942 Cripps Mission’s failure and set the stage for the 1945 Labour government’s commitment to transfer power.

The 1946‑47 Interim Government’s Food Policy: Rationing, Procurement, and the 1946‑47 Grain Crisis

Context: Post‑War Shortages and the Legacy of the 1943 Bengal Famine

The Interim Government, sworn in on 2 September 1946, inherited a fragile food economy. The 1943 Bengal famine had exposed the dangers of speculative hoarding, inadequate procurement, and a transport system geared to military needs. By 1946, a poor monsoon in the wheat‑growing provinces of the Punjab and the United Provinces reduced the 1945‑46 rabi harvest by an estimated 12 %, while the 1946 kharif rice crop in Bengal and Bihar fell short by 8 %. Urban centres — Calcutta, Bombay, Delhi — faced acute price inflation, with wheat prices rising 45 % above pre‑war levels.

Statutory Rationing and Fair‑Price Shops

The Food Department of the Interim Government, acting under the Essential Supplies (Temporary Powers) Act, 1946, imposed statutory rationing in 12 major cities by November 1946. Each adult received a weekly entitlement of 350 grams of wheat or rice, distributed through a network of “Fair‑Price Shops” run by licensed dealers. The scheme was financed by a central procurement fund that bought grain at fixed procurement prices from surplus provinces (mainly the Punjab) and sold it at subsidised rates. The Foodgrains Policy Committee, chaired by the Food Member, monitored stocks and adjusted quotas monthly.

Procurement Challenges and the 1946‑47 Grain Crisis

Procurement targets were missed because of transport bottlenecks — the railway system, still under military priority, allocated only 30 % of wagon capacity to civilian grain movement. Hoarding by large landlords and traders, encouraged by expectations of higher prices after the imminent partition, further reduced market arrivals. By February 1947 the central buffer stock fell below the 2‑million‑ton safety threshold, prompting the Interim Government to appeal for emergency imports from Australia and Argentina. The crisis intensified communal tensions; food riots erupted in Calcutta (March 1947) and Delhi (April 1947), often overlapping with sectarian violence.

Policy Legacy

The experience shaped independent India’s first Five‑Year Plan, which prioritised the creation of the Food Corporation of India (1965) and a permanent Public Distribution System. The 1946‑47 episode demonstrated that statutory rationing, backed by a central procurement agency and a transparent fair‑price mechanism, could mitigate urban hunger even under severe supply constraints — provided transport and anti‑hoarding enforcement were effective.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Police and Paramilitary Forces

Partition Council’s Police Committee and Allocation Formula

The Partition Council, constituted under the Indian Independence Act, 1947, established a Police Committee chaired by the Home Member of the Interim Government. The committee adopted a dual allocation formula: (i) personnel were divided on the basis of religious composition of the force (Muslim officers opted for Pakistan, non‑Muslim for India) and (ii) physical assets — arms, vehicles, buildings — were split proportionally to the territorial division of each province. In Punjab, where the police strength was 48,000, the formula yielded roughly 28,000 for Pakistan and 20,000 for India after accounting for voluntary transfers.

Transfer of Provincial and Princely State Forces

Provincial police forces (Punjab, Bengal, Sind, North‑West Frontier, Assam, Bombay, Madras, United Provinces, Bihar, Central Provinces, Orissa) were reconstituted as the police services of the successor dominions. Princely state police forces — such as the Mysore State Police, the Hyderabad State Police, and the Travancore State Police — were integrated into the Indian Police Service after accession, while those in states acceding to Pakistan (e.g., Bahawalpur, Khairpur) joined the Pakistan Police. The integration required standardisation of ranks, pay scales, and training curricula, a process overseen by the newly formed All‑India Police Commission (1948).

Paramilitary Units: Frontier Constabulary, Assam Rifles, and the Punjab Border Force

The Frontier Constabulary (FC), recruited mainly from Pashtun tribes, was split along the Durand Line; the portion stationed in the North‑West Frontier Province became the Pakistan Frontier Constabulary, while the Indian segment was absorbed into the newly raised Indian Reserve Police. The Assam Rifles, tasked with guarding the Indo‑Burma frontier, remained under Indian command because the entire unit lay east of the Radcliffe Line. The Punjab Border Force, created ad‑hoc in August 1947 from Sikh and Dogra battalions, was disbanded by December 1947 after the bulk of population exchange concluded, with its personnel redistributed to the Punjab Armed Police (India) and the Punjab Constabulary (Pakistan).

Operational Challenges During Migration

During the mass migration of 1947‑48, police and paramilitary units were the primary agents of escort, camp security, and riot control. The Indian side deployed the newly formed Central Reserve Police (later CRPF) to guard refugee convoys on the Grand Trunk Road, while the Pakistan side relied on the Pakistan Rangers (raised from former Punjab Border Force elements). Coordination was hampered by the absence of a joint command structure; the Joint Defence Council’s Police Sub‑Committee met only sporadically. Casualties among police personnel exceeded 2,500 in the first six months, underscoring the human cost of partition‑era law‑and‑order duties.

Long‑Term Institutional Impact

The partition‑driven reorganisation established the precedent of a centrally recruited, all‑India police service (the Indian Police Service, 1948) distinct from provincial forces, and a parallel central paramilitary framework (CRPF, BSF, ITBP) that evolved from the emergency units of 1947. In Pakistan, the Police Service of Pakistan and the Frontier Corps followed a similar trajectory. The 1947 experience also entrenched the practice of deploying paramilitary forces for internal security, a feature that continues to shape both nations’ policing doctrines.

  • Confusing the Non‑Cooperation Movement’s suspension (1922) with the Civil Disobedience Movement’s suspension (1934); they were triggered by different incidents and led to different strategic shifts.
  • Attributing the 1942 Quit India Movement’s sabotage solely to Congress volunteers; parallel governments and local militias also conducted independent actions.
  • Assuming the 1946‑47 Interim Government’s food rationing covered the entire country; statutory rationing was limited to 12 major urban centres.
  • Overlooking the role of the Partition Council’s Police Committee in allocating assets; the division was not a simple 50‑50 split but followed a religious‑personnel and territorial‑asset formula.
  • Treating the Frontier Constabulary as a wholly Pakistani force after partition; a segment remained in India and was reorganised into the Indian Reserve Police.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise factual recall and contextual analysis.

The 1930‑31 Round Table Conferences: British Strategy, Indian Participation, and Outcomes

The British government convened three Round Table Conferences in London between November 1930 and December 1932 to discuss constitutional reforms after the Simon Commission’s failure. The first conference (12 November 1930 – 19 January 1931) was attended by 89 delegates — 58 from British India, 16 from princely states, and 15 from British political parties — but the Indian National Congress boycotted it while the Civil Disobedience Movement continued. The British aimed to show willingness to negotiate while retaining control over the agenda, focusing on federal structure, provincial autonomy, and minority safeguards.

The second conference (7 September – 1 December 1931) followed the Gandhi‑Irwin Pact (5 March 1931). Gandhi attended as the sole Congress representative, demanding full responsible government at the centre and the removal of communal electorates introduced by the 1909 and 1919 Acts. The Muslim League, the Princes, and the Depressed Classes (represented by B. R. Ambedkar) pressed for separate electorates and weightage. The conference deadlocked on the communal question, and the British issued a unilateral Communal Award in 1932.

The third conference (17 November – 24 December 1932) was attended by the Muslim League, the Princes, and minority representatives; Congress again boycotted. By this stage the British had already drafted the Government of India Bill. The conference served mainly to legitimise the bill’s provisions: a federal centre with limited powers, provincial autonomy, and a complex system of communal representation. The bill became the Government of India Act 1935, which provided the constitutional framework for the 1937 provincial elections and the subsequent Congress ministries.

Key Takeaways

  • Three conferences spaced over two years; only the second saw direct Congress participation.
  • British strategy: use conferences to shape a constitution that preserved imperial interests while appearing consultative.
  • Communal representation remained the principal stumbling block, leading to the 1932 Communal Award and the Poona Pact.
  • The 1935 Act, rooted in conference deliberations, introduced provincial autonomy and a federal scheme that never fully materialised.

The 1932 Communal Award and the Poona Pact: Electoral Mechanics and Dalit Representation

On 16 August 1932 Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald announced the Communal Award, extending separate electorates to the Depressed Classes (now Scheduled Castes) in addition to Muslims, Sikhs, Europeans, and Anglo‑Indians. The Award allocated 71 reserved seats for Depressed Classes in provincial legislatures, to be filled by voters registered exclusively as “Depressed Class” electors. This move was intended to protect minority interests but was perceived by Gandhi as a British attempt to fracture Hindu unity.

Gandhi began a fast‑unto‑death in Yerwada Jail on 20 September 1932, demanding the Award’s withdrawal for Depressed Classes. After intense negotiations, B. R. Ambedkar and Gandhi signed the Poona Pact on 24 September 1932. The Pact replaced separate electorates with a system of reserved seats: 148 seats in provincial legislatures (up from 71) and 18 % of seats in the central legislature, to be filled by a joint electorate in which all Hindus could vote, but only Depressed Class candidates could contest. The Pact also provided for a primary election among Depressed Class voters to select a panel of four candidates for each reserved seat.

The Poona Pact shaped the 1937 provincial elections, where Congress won the largest share of reserved seats (78 out of 151), and it established the template for post‑independence reservation policy. However, Ambedkar later criticised the Pact for denying the Depressed Classes an independent political voice, arguing that joint electorates allowed dominant caste Hindus to influence the choice of Dalit representatives.

Electoral Mechanics Compared

Communal Award vs. Poona Pact

Comparative Analysis of Electoral Provisions
Question: Compare the Communal Award and the Poona Pact in terms of electorate structure and seat allocation for Depressed Classes.
The Communal Award created a separate electorate for Depressed Classes, granting them 71 provincial seats elected exclusively by Depressed Class voters. The Poona Pact abolished that separate electorate, substituting a joint electorate where all Hindus voted, but reserved 148 provincial seats for Depressed Class candidates chosen through a primary panel of four nominees. At the centre, the Pact introduced an 18 % reservation absent from the Award. Thus, the Pact increased numerical representation while integrating Dalit voters into the general Hindu electorate, a shift that defined subsequent reservation policy.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about communal representation, always note the transition from separate electorates (Communal Award) to reserved seats within a joint electorate (Poona Pact). Remember the numbers: 71 → 148 provincial seats, and the introduction of 18 % central reservation. This contrast is a frequent source of MCQ distractors.

The 1940 August Offer: British War‑Time Proposal and Nationalist Rejection

On 8 August 1940 Viceroy Lord Linlithgow announced the August Offer, a British attempt to secure Indian cooperation in the Second World War. The Offer promised dominion status after the war, an expanded Viceroy’s Executive Council with a majority of Indian members, and a War Advisory Council comprising Indian leaders. Crucially, it reiterated that no constitutional change would be made without the agreement of “majority” and “minority” communities, implicitly recognising the Muslim League’s claim to speak for Muslims.

The Congress Working Committee rejected the Offer on 21 August 1940; Gandhi dismissed it as a “post‑dated cheque” that failed to guarantee immediate independence or a constituent assembly. Gandhi launched the Individual Satyagraha (October 1940 – January 1941), a limited, non‑violent protest in which selected individuals courted arrest to demonstrate that the Congress did not support the war effort. The Muslim League, while welcoming the acknowledgement of minority rights, insisted on an explicit commitment to Pakistan, which the Offer did not provide.

The Offer’s failure deepened the deadlock: the British refused to concede a constituent assembly, the Congress refused to cooperate without a clear timeline for self‑government, and the League hardened its demand for a separate Muslim state. The episode set the stage for the 1942 Cripps Mission, which attempted a more concrete proposal but likewise collapsed over the same communal fault‑lines.

Significance

  • First British wartime declaration linking post‑war dominion status to Indian cooperation.
  • Explicit recognition of minority veto, strengthening the League’s bargaining position.
  • Triggered Individual Satyagraha, keeping the nationalist movement alive under repression.
  • Demonstrated the limits of British “offer‑and‑reject” diplomacy in the face of irreconcilable nationalist aspirations.

The 1945 Simla Conference and the Wavell Plan: Negotiations, Deadlock, and Consequences

In June 1945 Viceroy Lord Wavell summoned 21 Indian leaders to Simla to discuss the formation of a new Executive Council that would govern until a permanent constitution could be framed. The Wavell Plan proposed an expanded council of 14 members: six Hindus, five Muslims, one Sikh, one Christian, and one Parsi, with the Viceroy retaining the defence portfolio. The plan envisaged parity between caste Hindus and Muslims, and it gave the Muslim League the right to nominate all Muslim members.

The Congress, led by Maulana Azad, objected to the League’s exclusive claim to Muslim representation, arguing that the Congress also represented a substantial Muslim constituency. The League, under Jinnah, refused to accept any Muslim member not nominated by it. After two weeks of talks (25 June – 14 July 1945) the conference broke down without agreement. Wavell then reconstituted the Executive Council with officials and a few non‑League Muslims, but the political vacuum persisted.

The failure of the Simla Conference had three major consequences. First, it confirmed the League’s veto over any interim arrangement, boosting its credibility ahead of the 1945‑46 provincial elections. Second, it forced the British to recognise that a negotiated settlement required League participation, paving the way for the 1946 Cabinet Mission. Third, it deepened Congress‑League mistrust, making the eventual partition negotiations more adversarial.

Key Points

  • Wavell Plan’s parity formula and League’s nomination right were the core sticking points.
  • Congress’s insistence on a nationalist Muslim presence clashed with the League’s “sole spokesman” claim.
  • Conference collapse accelerated the electoral contest of 1945‑46, which the League won decisively in Muslim‑majority provinces.
  • The episode illustrated the limits of British “top‑down” constitutional engineering without prior political consensus.

The 1947 Mountbatten Plan and the June 3 Plan: Final Negotiations, Acceptance, and Partition Mechanics

Admiral Lord Louis Mountbatten arrived as Viceroy on 24 March 1947 with a mandate to transfer power by June 1948. After rapid consultations, he advanced the deadline to 15 August 1947 and unveiled the Mountbatten Plan on 3 June 1947 (the June 3 Plan). The plan provided for the partition of Punjab and Bengal along religious‑majority lines, a referendum in the North‑West Frontier Province and the Sylhet district of Assam, and the creation of two sovereign dominions — India and Pakistan — with the British Parliament passing the Indian Independence Act to give legal effect.

The Congress Working Committee accepted the plan on 15 June 1947, despite reservations about the vivisection of the country, because it offered a clear, immediate transfer of power and avoided a prolonged civil war. The Muslim League accepted it on 9 June 1947, viewing it as the realisation of the Lahore Resolution. The plan’s operational mechanics were entrusted to the Partition Council, the Boundary Commission (chaired by Sir Cyril Radcliffe), and the Armed Forces Reconstitution Committee.

Implementation proceeded at breakneck speed: the Radcliffe Award was announced on 17 August 1947 (after independence), the division of assets, finances, and personnel followed the formulas agreed in the Partition Council, and the two dominions assumed sovereignty at midnight on 14‑15 August 1947. The haste left unresolved issues — notably the status of Kashmir, the division of river waters, and the rehabilitation of millions of refugees — that continue to shape sub‑continental politics.

Operational Highlights

  • Partition of Punjab and Bengal based on district‑level Muslim/non‑Muslim majorities.
  • Referendum in NWFP (voted to join Pakistan) and Sylhet (voted to join East Pakistan).
  • Indian Independence Act, 1947 passed by British Parliament on 18 July 1947, receiving royal assent on 18 July.
  • Transfer of power at midnight 14‑15 August 1947; Mountbatten remained Governor‑General of India until June 1948.
  • Boundary Commission’s award delayed, causing massive displacement and violence.
  • Confusing the three Round Table Conferences’ chronology and the level of Congress participation in each.
  • Assuming the Communal Award granted separate electorates to all minorities; it applied specifically to Depressed Classes, Muslims, Sikhs, Europeans, and Anglo‑Indians.
  • Believing the Poona Pact abolished reserved seats; it actually increased them from 71 to 148 and introduced a joint electorate with a primary panel.
  • Thinking the August Offer promised immediate independence; it only promised dominion status after the war and retained a minority veto.
  • Overlooking that the Simla Conference failed primarily over the Muslim League’s claim to nominate all Muslim members of the Executive Council.
  • Assuming the Mountbatten Plan was accepted unanimously; several Congress leaders, including Gandhi, expressed deep reservations.
  • Mixing up the dates of the June 3 Plan (3 June 1947) and the Indian Independence Act (royal assent 18 July 1947).

Congress Ministries’ Legislative Record (1937‑39): Tenancy Reform, Labour Laws, and Civil Liberties

The Congress ministries formed after the 1937 provincial elections under the Government of India Act 1935 operated in eight provinces — Bombay, Madras, United Provinces, Bihar, Central Provinces, Orissa, Assam, and the North‑West Frontier Province. Despite the overriding authority of the governors, the ministries used their legislative majorities to push a reform agenda that reflected the Congress’s social‑economic programme. In the agrarian sphere, the Bombay Tenancy Act (1938) conferred occupancy rights on long‑standing tenants, regulated rent, and provided for compulsory purchase of land by tenants. The United Provinces Tenancy Act (1939) extended similar protections, while the Bihar Tenancy Act (1938) and the Central Provinces Tenancy Act (1938) introduced security of tenure and regulated rent. In Madras, the Temple Entry Authorization Act (1939) legally sanctioned the entry of Dalits into major temples, a legislative follow‑up to the Vaikom and Guruvayur satyagrahas.

Labour legislation was another priority. The Bombay Industrial Disputes Act (1938) established conciliation boards and compulsory arbitration for industrial disputes, and the Bombay Trade Unions Act (1938) granted legal recognition to unions. Madras passed the Madras Trade Union Act (1938) and the Madras Industrial Disputes Act (1939), while the Central Provinces enacted the Central Provinces Industrial Disputes Act (1939). These laws expanded the legal space for collective bargaining, though they retained provisions for government intervention in strikes deemed prejudicial to public order.

On civil liberties, the ministries repealed several repressive statutes: the Bombay Special Powers Act (1932) was withdrawn, political prisoners were released in Madras and the United Provinces, and press censorship was relaxed. However, the governors retained the power to veto bills, and financial dependence on the central government limited the scope of reform. The ministries resigned in November 1939 after the Viceroy declared India a belligerent in the Second World War without consulting Indian leaders, ending a brief but consequential experiment in provincial self‑government.

The 1939‑45 War Economy: Defence Production, Industrial Licensing, and the Managing Agency Houses

The outbreak of the Second World War transformed India into a major supply base for the Allied war effort. The colonial state invoked the Defence of India Act (1939) and a series of Defence of India Rules to impose comprehensive controls over production, pricing, and distribution. A War Supply Board was created in 1940, followed by the Department of Supply in 1941, to coordinate procurement of textiles, jute, steel, and non‑ferrous metals. Ordnance factories at Ishapore, Cossipore, and Kanpur were expanded, and new facilities such as Hindustan Aircraft Limited (Bangalore, 1940) and Indian Telephone Industries (Bangalore, 1943) were established under state auspices.

Industrial licensing became the principal instrument of directed production. A 1940 industrial policy resolution reserved key sectors — arms, aircraft, shipbuilding, and telecommunications — for the state, while private enterprises required licences for capacity expansion. The Essential Commodities Act (1943) empowered the centre to control prices, regulate stockpiling, and ration critical inputs such as coal, cement, and chemicals. Managing agency houses — notably the Tatas, Birlas, and the Martin Burn group — leveraged their existing capital and managerial networks to secure lucrative war contracts. Tata Steel supplied armour plate; Birla’s jute mills produced sandbags and sacking; and the Walchand group entered aircraft manufacturing through Hindustan Aircraft.

The war also accelerated the growth of a nascent public sector. A 1944 planning committee (chaired by Sir M. Visvesvaraya) recommended state‑led industrialisation, influencing the post‑war Industrial Policy Resolution of 1948. However, wartime controls created distortions: black markets flourished, real wages fell, and the 1943 Bengal famine exposed the fragility of the procurement‑rationing system. The managing agencies emerged from the war with enlarged assets and political clout, shaping the industrial landscape of independent India.

Examiner's trap
Which of the following was a feature of the wartime industrial licensing regime in India (1939‑45)?
The wartime regime reserved strategic sectors (arms, aircraft, shipbuilding, telecommunications) for the state and required private firms to obtain licences for any capacity expansion. It did not abolish private enterprise, nor did it introduce a comprehensive five‑year planning framework — that came after independence. The correct answer is the reservation of key sectors for state control combined with mandatory licensing for private expansion.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks about the war economy, distinguish between the colonial state’s ad‑hoc wartime controls (Defence of India Rules, Essential Commodities Act) and the post‑war planned economy (1948 Industrial Policy, Planning Commission). Look for keywords such as “licensing”, “reservation”, “managing agency”, and “ordnance factories” to identify the wartime context.

Civil Disobedience in the Princely States: Mysore, Travancore, and Hyderabad (1930‑34)

The Civil Disobedience Movement (1930‑34) was not confined to British India; it resonated in several princely states where local Congress units and parallel organisations mobilised against feudal exactions and denial of civil rights. In Mysore, the Mysore Congress, formed in 1930, launched a forest‑satyagraha against the state’s restrictive forest laws that curtailed tribal access to minor forest produce. The Diwan, Sir Mirza Ismail, responded with arrests and press censorship, but the agitation forced the state to amend the Forest Act in 1933, granting limited concessions.

In Travancore, the movement intertwined with the temple‑entry campaign. The Kerala Provincial Congress Committee organised a satyagraha at the Guruvayur temple in 1931‑32 demanding entry for Dalits. The Travancore government initially resisted but eventually issued the Temple Entry Proclamation (1936), a landmark that preceded similar reforms in British India. The civil disobedience phase also saw a boycott of government schools and courts, and the establishment of national schools in Malabar.

Hyderabad presented a more repressive environment. The Nizam’s government banned the Andhra Mahasabha and the Hyderabad State Congress (formed 1938) and imposed the Hyderabad Public Security Act (1935) to curb political activity. Nevertheless, the Andhra Mahasabha organised a peasant satyagraha in the Telangana region (1930‑31) against the vetti (forced labour) system and excessive land revenue. The movement was brutally suppressed, but it laid the organisational groundwork for the later Telangana armed struggle (1946‑51). In all three states, the absence of responsible government meant that civil disobedience operated under severe legal constraints, yet it succeeded in extracting limited reforms and in politicising new social groups.

Partition and the Reorganisation of Higher Education: University Splits, Faculty Migration, and the Creation of New Institutions (1947‑1948)

The partition of Punjab and Bengal necessitated the division of university assets and the relocation of academic staff. The University of the Punjab, headquartered in Lahore, was split: the East Punjab University (later renamed Panjab University) was established in 1947 at Shimla, later moving to Chandigarh, while the original university continued in Lahore as the University of the Punjab (Pakistan). The University of Dhaka remained in East Pakistan, but a significant number of Hindu faculty and students migrated to Calcutta, bolstering the University of Calcutta and prompting the expansion of institutions like Jadavpur University (formally established 1955, which grew from institutions expanded during the refugee influx).

Aligarh Muslim University, though located in Uttar Pradesh, experienced a sharp decline in Muslim student enrolment as many opted for Pakistan; the university nonetheless remained a central institution in India. The Inter‑University Board (later the Association of Indian Universities) coordinated the transfer of library collections, laboratory equipment, and examination records. The Government of India set up a University Grants Committee in 1948 to oversee the allocation of central assistance to the newly formed universities.

Faculty migration reshaped disciplinary strengths. Physicists such as D. S. Kothari moved to Delhi, contributing to the founding of the Defence Science Laboratory. Economists and statisticians from Dhaka joined the Indian Statistical Institute in Calcutta. The immediate post‑partition period also saw the establishment of the Indian Institute of Technology at Kharagpur (1951) as a direct response to the perceived need for technical manpower, but its planning began in 1946 under the Sarkar Committee, which accounted for the loss of technical institutions in the western wing. The reorganisation thus combined asset division, human‑capital redistribution, and the seeding of new national institutions.

Partition and the Public Health Crisis: Disease Surveillance, Refugee Camps, and the Reorganisation of Medical Services (1947‑1948)

The mass migration of an estimated 14‑16 million people across the new borders created a public health emergency of unprecedented scale. Refugee camps in Delhi, Punjab, and West Bengal became epicentres for cholera, smallpox, dysentery, and malaria. The Indian Medical Service, supplemented by the Indian Red Cross Society and the newly formed Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS) under the Ministry of Health, mounted large‑scale vaccination drives: millions of cholera inoculations and smallpox vaccinations were administered between August 1947 and March 1948.

Disease surveillance was reorganised through the establishment of the Central Malaria Organisation (1947) and the expansion of epidemic intelligence services, which compiled weekly morbidity reports from camp dispensaries. The government requisitioned military hospitals and converted schools and factories into temporary isolation wards. A substantial emergency medical budget was allocated for emergency medical relief, covering drugs, disinfectants, and the transport of medical personnel.

The crisis accelerated the integration of provincial health departments into a more unified national framework. The 1948 Health Survey and Development Committee (Bhore Committee) submitted its report in 1946, but its recommendations for a comprehensive primary health care network gained urgency after partition. The central government began drafting the Employees’ State Insurance Act (1948) and the Central Government Health Scheme (1954) as part of a longer‑term strategy to provide organised medical care to the displaced and the broader population. The partition experience thus directly shaped the architecture of India’s post‑colonial public health system.

  • Confusing the legislative achievements of Congress ministries with the later post‑independence land reforms; the 1937‑39 acts were provincial and limited by governor’s veto.
  • Attributing the wartime industrial licensing system to the post‑war Planning Commission; the licensing regime operated under the Defence of India Rules, not the Industries (Development and Regulation) Act.
  • Assuming civil disobedience in princely states mirrored the British‑India pattern; princely states lacked responsible government and used distinct repressive laws such as the Hyderabad Public Security Act.
  • Believing the University of the Punjab was wholly transferred to Pakistan; the East Punjab University was created in India from the same assets.
  • Overlooking the role of the Directorate General of Health Services in the 1947‑48 epidemic response; it was a new central body, not a continuation of the pre‑war Indian Medical Service alone.
  • Treating the 1947‑48 public health measures as a fully planned national programme; they were emergency improvisations that later informed the Bhore Committee’s recommendations.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Civil Aviation and Air Transport Network

Pre‑Partition Aviation Landscape

Before 1947 civil aviation in British India operated under the Indian Aircraft Act of 1934 and was dominated by a handful of private carriers — Tata Airlines (later Air India), Indian National Airways, and several regional operators such as Himalayan Aviation and Deccan Airways. The Directorate General of Civil Aviation (DGCA), established in 1927 under the Department of Industries and Labour, regulated licensing, airworthiness, and route approvals. Major airports at Calcutta (Dum Dum), Bombay (Santacruz), Delhi (Palam), Karachi (Mauripur), and Lahore (Walton) formed the backbone of the domestic network, while international services linked India with the United Kingdom, the Middle East, and Southeast Asia through Imperial Airways and later BOAC.

Asset Division and the Birth of Two National Carriers

The Partition Council’s Transport Committee allocated aircraft, hangars, and navigation aids on a population‑and‑revenue basis. Of the roughly 80 serviceable aircraft registered in India at August 1947, about 55 were assigned to the Dominion of India and 25 to Pakistan. Tata Airlines, headquartered in Bombay, retained its fleet of Douglas DC‑3s and became the nucleus of Air India International (incorporated 1948). In Pakistan, the government nationalised the assets of Orient Airways and merged them with the former Indian National Airways fleet to create Pakistan International Airlines (PIA) in 1955, but the immediate post‑partition carrier was the Pakistan Air Transport Corporation formed in 1948.

Airport Re‑Classification and Infrastructure Gaps

Key airports fell on opposite sides of the Radcliffe Line. Karachi’s Mauripur Airport, the premier international gateway, went to Pakistan, forcing India to accelerate development of Bombay’s Santacruz and Delhi’s Palam for long‑haul operations. The 1948‑49 budget earmarked substantial funds for runway extensions at Palam and the construction of a new terminal at Santacruz. In the east, Calcutta’s Dum Dum remained with India, but the loss of Chittagong and Dacca airfields required new routes to Assam and the North‑East, prompting the 1950 “North‑East Air Link” programme.

Regulatory Continuity and the 1953 Air Corporations Act

The DGCA continued to function under the same statutory framework until the Air Corporations Act of 1953 nationalised all scheduled airlines, creating Indian Airlines for domestic routes and Air India International for overseas services. The Act also clarified regulatory oversight, keeping the DGCA as the safety regulator. The 1953 legislation used the 1947 asset division as a reference for capital allocation between the two new corporations.

Operational Challenges During Migration

During the mass migration of 1947‑48, civil aircraft were requisitioned for refugee airlifts. The Indian Air Force’s transport wing, supplemented by chartered DC‑3s, flew over a lakh passengers between Punjab and Delhi between August and November 1947. The operation exposed shortages of ground crew, fuel, and navigational charts, prompting the 1948 “Air Transport Emergency Regulations” that granted the DGCA powers to commandeer private aircraft and fix fares.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Banking Sector: Commercial Banks, Cooperative Credit, and the Reserve Bank’s Expanded Mandate

Pre‑Partition Banking Structure

At independence the Indian banking system comprised nearly 100 scheduled commercial banks, over a thousand non‑scheduled banks, and a dense network of cooperative credit societies governed by the Cooperative Societies Act of 1912. The Reserve Bank of India (RBI), established under the RBI Act of 1934, acted as the central bank for the whole of British India, issuing currency, managing the exchange rate, and supervising scheduled banks through the Banking Companies Act of 1949 (enacted after partition but based on 1947 drafts).

Division of Assets and Liabilities

The Partition Council’s Finance Committee, with RBI Governor Sir Chintaman Deshmukh playing a key role, applied a “branch‑wise” formula: each bank’s assets and liabilities were split in proportion to the number of branches located in the two dominions. Of the scheduled banks, the majority had branches only in India, a minority only in Pakistan, and around a dozen operated on both sides. The dual‑presence banks — notably the Imperial Bank of India, the Punjab National Bank, and the Central Bank of India — transferred thousands of branches and deposits worth hundreds of crores to Pakistan. The Imperial Bank’s extensive branch network in West Punjab and Sindh became the nucleus of the newly formed National Bank of Pakistan in 1949.

Currency Separation and the 1948 Monetary Agreement

Until 1948 the Indian rupee remained legal tender in Pakistan. The 1948 Monetary Agreement, signed by the RBI and the State Bank of Pakistan (established 1 July 1948), provided for a phased withdrawal: Indian notes were demonetised in Pakistan by 30 June 1949, while Pakistan issued its own rupee at parity. The RBI retained the sole right to issue currency for India, and the agreement mandated a clearing mechanism for inter‑dominion trade settlements through a London‑based clearing arrangement until bilateral payment arrangements matured.

Cooperative Credit Re‑Organisation

Cooperative societies were provincially registered; the partition split the Punjab Cooperative Bank and the Bengal Central Cooperative Bank into two entities each. The Indian side retained the Punjab State Cooperative Bank (re‑registered 1948) and the West Bengal Cooperative Bank. The RBI issued the “Cooperative Credit (Partition) Directions, 1948” requiring societies to submit revised bye‑laws, reconstitute managing committees, and re‑capitalise within six months. By 1950 the number of primary agricultural credit societies in India rose from around 12,000 to over 18,000, reflecting the influx of refugee farmers who were granted priority loans under the 1949 “Refugee Rehabilitation Loan Scheme”.

Regulatory Expansion: The 1949 Banking Companies Act

Although enacted after partition, the Banking Companies Act of 1949 was drafted in 1947 to address the regulatory vacuum created by the division. It empowered the RBI to license banks, inspect books, and enforce capital adequacy (minimum paid‑up capital ₹5 lakh for scheduled banks). The Act also introduced liquid asset requirements that evolved into the Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR), a tool that became central to monetary policy in the 1950s. The RBI’s expanded mandate included the supervision of the newly formed State Bank of India (1955), which absorbed the Imperial Bank’s Indian operations.

Technique: Comparative Asset Allocation
A question asks: “Explain how the branch‑wise formula used for dividing scheduled bank assets in 1947 differed from the population‑based formula applied to civil aviation assets.”
The branch‑wise formula allocated each bank’s assets and liabilities in proportion to the number of its branches situated in each dominion, preserving the commercial logic of each institution’s existing network. In contrast, the Transport Committee allocated aircraft and airport infrastructure on a population‑and‑revenue basis, reflecting the strategic nature of aviation as a public utility rather than a purely commercial enterprise. The former respected corporate continuity; the latter prioritised sovereign capacity to operate international routes.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question compares two partition‑era asset divisions, identify the governing committee (Finance vs Transport), the metric used (branches vs population‑revenue), and the policy objective (commercial continuity vs strategic sovereignty). Tabulate these three dimensions in a quick mental table; it yields a concise, high‑scoring answer.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Judicial System: High Courts, the Federal Court, and the Emergence of the Supreme Court

Pre‑Partition Judicial Hierarchy

Under the Government of India Act 1935 the judicial apex was the Federal Court of India (established 1937) with original, appellate, and advisory jurisdiction. Below it stood the High Courts of Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, Allahabad, Lahore, Patna, and the Chief Court of Oudh, each exercising original and appellate powers over their respective provinces. The Privy Council in London remained the final court of appeal for Indian cases until the Federal Court’s appellate jurisdiction was expanded in 1949.

Territorial Re‑Assignment of High Courts

The Radcliffe Award placed the Lahore High Court and the Chief Court of Sindh (Karachi) in Pakistan. Consequently, the Indian Dominion retained the High Courts of Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, Allahabad, Patna, and the newly created East Punjab High Court (established 1947 at Shimla, later shifted to Chandigarh). The 1947 “High Courts (Partition) Order” transferred the records, pending cases, and judicial officers of the Lahore High Court to the new East Punjab High Court, while the Sindh Chief Court’s records moved to the Karachi High Court in Pakistan.

Federal Court Transition to the Supreme Court

The Indian Independence Act 1947 provided that the Federal Court would continue until a new Supreme Court was constituted under the forthcoming Constitution. The Constituent Assembly’s Judiciary Committee, chaired by Sir Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar, drafted Article 124 establishing the Supreme Court of India with a Chief Justice and up to seven other judges. The Federal Court’s last sitting was on 26 January 1950; its judges (including Chief Justice Sir H. J. Kania) were appointed as the first Supreme Court bench, ensuring institutional continuity.

Jurisdictional Adjustments and the 1949 Federal Court (Enlargement of Jurisdiction) Act

To bridge the gap between 1947 and 1950, the Federal Court (Enlargement of Jurisdiction) Act 1949 expanded the Federal Court’s appellate jurisdiction to include appeals from the High Courts of East Punjab and the newly formed High Court of Assam (created 1948). It also empowered the Federal Court to hear references from the Governor‑General on constitutional questions arising from the partition, such as the validity of the 1948 “Refugee Rehabilitation Ordinance”.

Impact on Legal Profession and Case Law

Over a thousand advocates enrolled with the Lahore High Court migrated to India; many were enrolled with the East Punjab High Court under a special “migration enrolment” scheme. The Supreme Court’s early jurisprudence (e.g., A.K. Gopalan v. State of Madras, 1950) drew heavily on Federal Court precedents, particularly a leading 1948 Federal Court ruling on preventive detention under the Defence of India Act. The partition thus shaped the doctrinal foundation of post‑colonial Indian constitutional law.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Scientific Research Establishment: Laboratories, Personnel, and the Birth of National Institutes

Pre‑Partition Research Landscape

Scientific research in British India was concentrated in the Indian Institute of Science (IISc, Bangalore, 1909), the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR, founded 1942), and a network of provincial laboratories — the Harcourt Butler Technological Institute (Kanpur), the Central Leather Research Institute (Madras), the National Chemical Laboratory (Pune, sanctioned 1947), and the Indian Agricultural Research Institute (Pusa, Bihar). The Royal Indian Navy and the Indian Army also maintained research wings for metallurgy and ballistics.

Personnel Migration and the “Brain Drain” to Pakistan

A notable proportion of CSIR’s scientific staff opted for Pakistan, including the physicist Dr. Rafi Muhammad Chaudhry, who later led Pakistan’s atomic energy programme. Conversely, India received an influx of Muslim scientists from Punjab and Bengal who chose to stay, notably Dr. M. S. Swaminathan, then a young researcher at IARI, and Dr. Vikram Sarabhai (who returned from Cambridge in 1947). The 1948 “Scientific Personnel Rehabilitation Scheme” provided travel grants and temporary appointments in Indian laboratories for displaced researchers.

Asset Division of Laboratories

The Partition Council’s Scientific Committee allocated laboratory equipment on a “project‑ownership” basis. The newly sanctioned National Chemical Laboratory at Pune remained in India; the planned Central Fuel Research Institute at Dhanbad stayed with India, while a petroleum research institute planned for Karachi was transferred to Pakistan and became part of PCSIR. The Indian Institute of Science’s library and instrument collections were untouched, but the “Indian Institute of Technology” planning committee (Sarkar Committee, 1946) saw its original proposal for a northern campus at Lahore abandoned after partition, accelerating the establishment of the first IIT at Kharagpur.

Establishment of New National Institutes

The perceived deficit in high‑technology capacity after partition accelerated the creation of the Indian Institutes of Technology (IITs). The first IIT at Kharagpur was inaugurated in 1951 on the former Hijli Detention Camp site, with initial funding from the Ministry of Education and technical assistance from the United States under the “Technical Cooperation Mission”. Simultaneously, the Atomic Energy Commission (AEC) was set up in 1948 under Dr. Homi J. Bhabha, leveraging the Tata Institute of Fundamental Research (TIFR, 1945) as its research base. The 1950 “National Physical Laboratory” (NPL) in New Delhi was modelled on the UK’s NPL and tasked with standards and metrology, filling the vacuum left by the loss of the Lahore standards laboratory.

Long‑Term Institutional Legacy

The partition‑driven reorganisation entrenched a centralised, state‑led research model. CSIR’s budget expanded significantly, from a few crores in 1947 to over ten crores by 1955, reflecting the priority accorded to self‑reliance in defence, agriculture, and industrial technology. The migration of scientists also shaped disciplinary strengths: physics and chemistry consolidated at TIFR and IISc, while agricultural genetics concentrated at IARI, later contributing to the Green Revolution of the 1960s.

  • Confusing the branch‑wise banking division with the population‑based aviation allocation; the two committees used distinct metrics and policy goals.
  • Assuming the Federal Court automatically became the Supreme Court on 15 August 1947; the transition required the 1949 Enlargement Act and the 1950 Constitution.
  • Overlooking the 1948 Monetary Agreement’s role in currency separation; the rupee remained common legal tender until mid‑1949.
  • Treating the 1947 partition of scientific laboratories as a simple geographic split; many projects were reassigned by ownership rather than location.
  • Believing the first IIT was a direct partition response; its planning began in 1946, but the loss of the Lahore campus accelerated its siting at Kharagpur.
  • Neglecting the special “migration enrolment” scheme for advocates; it was a unique administrative measure not replicated for other professions.
  • Assuming the Reserve Bank’s expanded mandate under the 1949 Banking Companies Act applied retrospectively to 1947; the Act was enacted later but drafted with partition in mind.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Meteorological Department

The Indian Meteorological Department (IMD) entered 1947 with a network of regional centres — Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, Lahore and Karachi — and a central headquarters that had moved from Simla to Pune in 1928. Upper‑air observatories at Srinagar, Lahore, Karachi and Dhaka supplied radiosonde data for monsoon forecasting, while a chain of surface stations fed the daily weather bulletins used by the railways, the army and the agrarian economy. The Partition Council’s Meteorological Committee met in mid‑1947 to allocate assets on a functional basis: the Lahore and Karachi centres, together with upper‑air stations in the western wing, were assigned to the newly created Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD). India retained the Pune headquarters, the Calcutta, Bombay and Madras centres, and the upper‑air station at Srinagar (which became accessible after the accession of Jammu & Kashmir in October 1947).

The division of climatological archives proved more contentious than the physical stations. The IMD’s historical rainfall series for the Punjab and Sind basins, essential for irrigation planning, were split by the Radcliffe Line. A bilateral agreement negotiated under the Inter‑Dominion Council provided for the exchange of monthly rainfall summaries and joint publication of climatological tables for several years. Personnel migration mirrored the asset split: a substantial number of gazetted officers opted for Pakistan, including senior synoptic meteorologists who formed the core of the PMD. India absorbed the remaining cadre and launched a recruitment drive to rebuild strength by 1950.

Operational consequences were immediate. The loss of the Karachi radiosonde station degraded western monsoon surveillance, prompting India to commission a new upper‑air unit at New Delhi and to acquire radar wind‑profilers through technical cooperation programmes. Pakistan faced the challenge of building a forecasting infrastructure from a fragmented inheritance; its early plans allocated resources for a network of surface observatories and a central forecasting office in Karachi. Both nations joined the World Meteorological Organization in the early 1950s, which standardised data‑exchange protocols and eased tensions over real‑time synoptic charts.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Survey of India

The Survey of India (SoI), headquartered at Dehradun since 1905, operated through five circles in 1947: Northern (Delhi), Eastern (Calcutta), Western (Bombay), Southern (Madras) and North‑Western (Peshawar). Its crown jewel was the Great Trigonometrical Survey (GTS) archive, containing the primary triangulation network that underpinned all cadastral and topographic mapping. The Partition Council’s Survey Committee allocated the Northern Circle (headquartered at Lahore) and the North‑Western Circle to the newly formed Survey of Pakistan (SoP), while India retained Dehradun and the three remaining circles. The 1:50,000 scale topographic sheets covering the Punjab and Bengal border districts were physically divided along the Radcliffe Award, with each dominion receiving the sheets falling within its territory.

Personnel and instrument pools followed the same logic. Several hundred gazetted surveyors transferred to Pakistan, including senior geodesists who became the founding leadership of the SoP. India’s Surveyor‑General oversaw the integration of the remaining staff and the re‑calibration of the GTS baseline at Dehradun. A bilateral Survey Cooperation Agreement provided for the exchange of geodetic control points along the new border, enabling the joint computation of boundary pillars that demarcate the international border in the Kashmir sector. The partition also accelerated institutional modernisation. India established the Indian Institute of Surveying and Mapping (IISM) at Hyderabad to train officers in photogrammetry and aerial survey, technologies demonstrated during aerial photography of the Punjab plains. Pakistan, with British technical assistance under the Colombo Plan, set up its own photogrammetric unit in Rawalpindi. By the mid‑1950s both organisations had produced revised 1:250,000 scale national atlas sheets, which became the cartographic basis for infrastructure projects in India and the Indus Basin irrigation scheme in Pakistan.

Examiner's technique: Comparative asset allocation
Compare the criteria used by the Partition Council’s Meteorological Committee and Survey Committee for dividing scientific assets. Why did the Meteorological Committee adopt a “functional” basis while the Survey Committee used a “territorial” basis?
The Meteorological Committee prioritised continuity of forecasting services; upper‑air stations and radiosonde networks were indivisible operational units, so allocation followed the functional need of each dominion to maintain a viable synoptic network. The Survey Committee, by contrast, dealt with static cartographic products — map sheets and triangulation control points — that are inherently tied to geographic coordinates. Therefore a territorial split along the Radcliffe Line ensured that each dominion possessed the maps and control data for its own territory, avoiding the impracticality of sharing a single topographic sheet across a new international border.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for “administrative reorganisation after partition”, structure the answer by institution: (1) pre‑partition structure, (2) Partition Council committee and its allocation principle, (3) asset and personnel division, (4) post‑partition institutional development, (5) long‑term impact on planning or security. This template works for Meteorology, Survey, Forest, Customs and any other technical department.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Forest Service

The Imperial Forest Service managed forest circles corresponding loosely to provincial boundaries: Punjab, Sind, Bengal, Assam, Central Provinces, Madras, Bombay and the United Provinces. The Partition Council’s Forest Committee divided the circles on a territorial basis. The Punjab Circle was split into East Punjab (India) and West Punjab (Pakistan), with the forest research station at Changa Manga transferring to Pakistan. The Bengal Circle was similarly divided; the Sundarbans mangrove reserve was split, with the larger portion falling to East Pakistan. The Assam Circle lost the Sylhet district after the 1947 referendum, transferring Sylhet’s tea‑garden forests to East Pakistan.

Personnel migration was substantial. Several hundred Imperial Forest Service officers opted for Pakistan, including senior conservators who formed the core of Pakistan’s new forest hierarchy. India retained the Forest Research Institute (FRI) at Dehradun, the Imperial Forest College (later the Indian Forest College) and the central library of silvicultural literature. Pakistan established the Pakistan Forest Institute at Peshawar and created a separate Forest Service cadre. A bilateral agreement on forest research exchange allowed the sharing of provenance trial data for teak, sal and chir pine, which proved critical for afforestation programmes in both countries.

The partition also reshaped wildlife conservation. The Indian Board for Wildlife (IBWL) was constituted in 1952, drawing on the pre‑partition network of game reserves now split across the border — notably Kaziranga and Manas (India) and the Sundarbans (Pakistan). Pakistan’s Wildlife Department, formed in the early 1950s, inherited the game reserves of the former North‑Western Frontier Province and the Punjab. An agreement on the protection of migratory birds was an early instance of trans‑boundary ecological cooperation. In India, an amendment to the Indian Forest Act 1927 introduced stricter controls on forest produce transit, a direct response to timber smuggling across the new Punjab border.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Customs and Excise Administration

Before partition, the Central Board of Revenue (CBR) administered customs under the Indian Customs Act 1934 and central excise under the Central Excises and Salt Act 1944. Major customs houses operated at Bombay, Calcutta, Karachi, Madras and Chittagong, while inland excise collectorates supervised the production of salt, sugar, textiles and tobacco. The Partition Council’s Finance Committee allocated the Karachi and Chittagong customs houses, together with associated warehousing and preventive staff, to Pakistan. India retained Bombay, Calcutta and Madras, and inherited the central excise factories in the western and eastern zones.

The division of revenue records and pending assessments required a special sub‑committee that met for several months. It transferred assessment files, preventive officers and revenue cutters stationed at Karachi to the newly created Pakistan Central Board of Revenue. India’s CBR was reorganised, with a new Directorate of Inspection established in New Delhi. A bilateral Customs Agreement provided for duty‑free transit of goods destined for the other dominion through designated land customs stations and laid down a joint valuation committee to resolve disputes over the classification of jute, cotton and tea.

The fiscal impact was immediate. Pakistan’s loss of the major customs revenue from Bombay and Calcutta forced the new government to raise import tariffs in its early budgets. India faced a shortfall in excise revenue from jute mills now located in East Pakistan; a trade agreement compensated by granting India a quota of raw jute at concessional rates. The coordinated devaluation of the Indian rupee and the Pakistani rupee in 1951 triggered a surge in smuggling across the Punjab border, prompting India to create the Directorate of Revenue Intelligence (DRI) in the late 1950s and Pakistan to establish its own Anti‑Smuggling Wing. These bodies became the institutional ancestors of the modern customs intelligence apparatus in both countries.

  • Confusing the functional allocation of meteorological stations with the territorial split of survey map sheets; the two committees used fundamentally different principles.
  • Assuming the Forest Research Institute at Dehradun was divided; it remained wholly in India while Pakistan built a new institute at Peshawar.
  • Overlooking the 1948 Indo‑Pakistan Customs Agreement’s role in establishing transit corridors; the agreement preceded the 1950 trade pact and shaped early revenue flows.
  • Believing the Survey of India’s Great Trigonometrical Survey archive was duplicated; only the control points on each side of the border were exchanged, not the entire dataset.
  • Neglecting the bilateral agreement on forest research exchange; it enabled joint provenance trials that underpinned the 1950s afforestation drives in both dominions.
  • Treating the 1951 rupee devaluation as an isolated Indian decision; Pakistan devalued simultaneously, and the coordinated move was negotiated through the Commonwealth Finance Ministers’ meeting.

The 1942 Quit India Movement’s Underground Radio Network: Congress Radio, Azad Hind Radio, and British Counter‑Propaganda

When the Congress leadership was arrested on 9 August 1942, the movement lost its public voice. Within weeks a covert broadcasting operation — known as Congress Radio — began transmitting from hidden locations in Bombay, Poona, and later from the forests of the Satara district. The station was organised by Usha Mehta, a student activist, with technical assistance from Nariman Printer and a small team of engineers who assembled a low‑power short‑wave transmitter from salvaged parts. Broadcasts aired at scheduled evening hours, carrying Gandhi’s “Do or Die” message, news of parallel governments, and operational guidance for sabotage activities. The station frequently changed frequencies and call‑signs to evade the Directorate of Wireless, which deployed direction‑finding equipment and a network of listening posts across the Deccan.

Simultaneously, Azad Hind Radio operated from Berlin (1942‑43) and later from Rangoon and Singapore under Subhas Chandra Bose’s Provisional Government of Free India. Its programmes, in Hindustani, Bengali, Tamil, and English, targeted Indian soldiers in the British Indian Army and civilian audiences in the eastern provinces. The Berlin station used a high‑power transmitter supplied by German authorities; the Southeast Asian stations relied on Japanese‑provided broadcasting facilities. Content combined nationalist rhetoric with Japanese war‑aims propaganda, a blend that British censors labelled “enemy‑inspired subversion”.

The British response was two‑pronged. All India Radio (AIR), the state‑run network, expanded its Hindi and Urdu services, inserting “loyalist” commentaries and rebuttals to Congress Radio’s claims. The Directorate of Military Intelligence ran a “Radio Monitoring Unit” that recorded Congress Radio’s transmissions, analysed call‑sign patterns, and coordinated raids on suspected transmitter sites. In November 1942 a raid on a farmhouse near Satara seized the Congress Radio equipment and arrested Mehta; regular broadcasting ceased soon after, with only sporadic transmissions reported later. Azad Hind Radio’s Berlin station fell silent after the German surrender in May 1945; its Southeast Asian outlets were dismantled as Japanese forces retreated.

The underground radio experience demonstrated that nationalist communication could survive total leadership decapitation, but also highlighted the limits of low‑power short‑wave in reaching the rural masses. Post‑war, the technical personnel trained in these operations contributed to India’s independent broadcasting infrastructure, informing the development of the All India Radio’s external services launched in 1948.

The 1945‑46 Provincial Elections: Electoral Mechanics, Muslim League Campaign Strategy, and the Mandate for Partition

The provincial elections of January‑February 1946 were held under the Government of India Act 1935, which retained separate electorates for Muslims, Sikhs, Europeans, and other minorities. The franchise was limited to approximately 10 % of the adult population, based on property, literacy, and tax‑paying qualifications. Each province elected a legislative assembly; the results determined the composition of the provincial ministries that would later negotiate the Cabinet Mission’s proposals.

The All‑India Muslim League contested the elections on a single‑point platform: the creation of Pakistan as a sovereign Muslim state. Its manifesto, drafted by the League’s Central Parliamentary Board, emphasised the “two‑nation theory”, demanded parity at the centre, and promised safeguards for Muslim culture, language, and economic interests. The League’s campaign machinery — organised through the Muslim League National Guard and a network of district‑level election committees — mobilised voters through Friday sermons, Urdu pamphlets, and the slogan “Pakistan Zindabad”. In Punjab and Bengal, the League drew on support from local land‑holding elites to secure rural Muslim votes.

The Indian National Congress fought on a platform of a united, secular India with a strong centre. Its 1945‑46 manifesto, approved by the Congress Working Committee, promised universal adult franchise after independence, land reforms, labour rights, and the abolition of princely privileges. Congress campaigners, however, faced organisational fatigue after the Quit India repression and the imprisonment of most senior leaders until mid‑1945. The party relied on the Congress Socialist Party cadre and the All‑India Kisan Sabha to reach peasant voters, but its appeal remained largely confined to the general (non‑Muslim) electorate.

Results gave the League a decisive majority of Muslim seats: over 400 out of approximately 490 Muslim seats across the provinces, including overwhelming majorities in Punjab, Bengal, and Sind. Congress won the vast majority of general seats but failed to win any Muslim seat outside the North‑West Frontier Province. The outcome was interpreted by the League as a popular mandate for partition, while Congress argued that the limited franchise and separate electorates distorted the popular will. The electoral arithmetic directly shaped the Cabinet Mission’s grouping scheme, as the League insisted on compulsory grouping of Muslim‑majority provinces — a demand rooted in the provincial election verdict.

Comparative Electoral Analysis
Compare the Muslim League’s 1945‑46 election manifesto with the Congress’s 1945‑46 manifesto in terms of communal representation and economic promises.
Answer: The League’s manifesto centred on communal representation — demanding separate electorates, parity at the centre, and a sovereign Muslim state — while offering vague economic assurances such as “protection of Muslim economic interests”. Congress’s manifesto rejected communal electorates, advocated joint electorates with universal adult franchise, and laid out concrete economic programmes: land‑reform legislation, minimum wages, workers’ right to strike, and nationalisation of key industries. The League’s appeal was identity‑based; Congress’s was class‑and‑citizenship‑based. Examiners often test the contrast between “communal safeguard” versus “secular economic reform” as the ideological fault line that made the 1946 elections a de‑facto referendum on partition.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “electoral mechanics”, list the franchise qualifications, separate electorate categories, and the seat‑distribution formula under the 1935 Act. Pair each mechanic with its political consequence (e.g., limited franchise → League’s ability to claim mandate despite low turnout). This two‑column mental table yields full marks for both description and analysis.

The 1946 Cabinet Mission’s Grouping Scheme: Detailed Mechanics of the Three‑Tier Federal Plan and Provincial Objections

The Cabinet Mission, arriving in March 1946, proposed a three‑tier federal structure to preserve Indian unity while accommodating Muslim‑majority aspirations. The plan divided British India into three Groups: Group A (Madras, Bombay, Central Provinces, United Provinces, Bihar, Orissa) — Hindu‑majority provinces; Group B (Punjab, North‑West Frontier Province, Sind, Baluchistan) — Muslim‑majority north‑west; Group C (Bengal, Assam) — Muslim‑majority east. Each Group would draft its own constitution for provincial and Group‑level subjects (education, health, agriculture), while the Union centre would control defence, foreign affairs, and communications.

A key feature was the “opt‑out” clause: after the first general elections under the new constitution, any province could, by a majority vote of its legislative assembly, secede from its Group. The Mission intended this as a safety valve, but the League interpreted it as a guarantee that Muslim‑majority Groups could eventually become independent. Congress, however, objected to the very principle of compulsory grouping, arguing that it entrenched communal divisions and violated the principle of a single sovereign constituent assembly. The Congress Working Committee characterised the grouping as a British device to perpetuate communalism.

The provincial reactions sharpened the deadlock. In Punjab, the Unionist Premier Khizar Hayat Khan Tiwana refused to accept Group B, fearing loss of Sikh and Hindu minority rights. In Bengal, the Muslim League ministry under H. S. Suhrawardy welcomed Group C but demanded that the opt‑out be exercised immediately. The Sikh leadership, represented by the Shiromani Akali Dal, demanded a separate Group D for the Punjab’s Sikh‑majority districts, a request the Mission rejected. The Mission’s attempt to mediate through a “Committee of Nine” (three each from Congress, League, and Sikhs) collapsed when the League insisted on compulsory grouping and Congress refused to participate in a grouped constituent assembly.

By June 1946 the Mission announced the failure of the grouping scheme and proposed an alternative: a single constituent assembly with a “sectional” voting procedure for Group‑level matters. This compromise also broke down, leading directly to the League’s call for Direct Action Day on 16 August 1946 and the subsequent communal violence that made partition appear inevitable.

The 1947 Partition and the Division of the Indian Scientific Research Establishment: CSIR, IISc, and the Atomic Energy Programme

At independence, the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR), founded in 1942, operated a network of national laboratories — including the National Chemical Laboratory (Pune), the National Physical Laboratory (Delhi), the Central Food Technological Research Institute (Mysore), and the Central Leather Research Institute (Madras). All laboratories were located in territory that became India; none fell in the new Pakistani provinces. Consequently, the entire CSIR infrastructure, its scientific staff, and its library holdings remained under Indian control. Pakistan established its own Pakistan Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (PCSIR) in 1953, initially staffed by a handful of scientists who migrated from Indian labs, and built new laboratories in Lahore, Karachi, and Dhaka.

The Indian Institute of Science (IISc), Bangalore, founded in 1909 with Tata endowment, remained wholly in India. Its departments of physics, chemistry, and engineering continued uninterrupted. The institute’s high‑voltage laboratory and the early cyclotron project (initiated in 1948) became the backbone of India’s nuclear physics research. No IISc assets were transferred to Pakistan.

The Atomic Energy Programme originated from the Tata Institute of Fundamental Research (TIFR), established in Bombay in 1945 under Homi J. Bhabha. TIFR’s cosmic‑ray and nuclear‑physics groups, together with the newly created Atomic Energy Commission (1948), formed the core of India’s nuclear effort. All TIFR facilities, the uranium‑processing pilot plant at Trombay (commissioned 1950), and the associated scientific personnel stayed in India. Pakistan’s nuclear programme began only in the 1950s with the formation of the Pakistan Atomic Energy Commission (1956) and the acquisition of a research reactor (PARR‑I) from the United States under the “Atoms for Peace” programme.

The division of scientific assets therefore followed a territorial continuity principle: institutions physically located in the Indian Union remained Indian; Pakistan had to create parallel institutions from scratch.
