The division of statistical personnel was modest; most ISI staff, including Mahalanobis, opted to stay in India, while a handful of demographers such as Mohammad Afzal moved to Karachi.

The first post‑partition census of India, conducted in 1951 under the Census Act of 1948, faced the unprecedented challenge of enumerating a population that had been reshaped by the migration of an estimated 14‑15 million people. The census adopted a de‑facto enumeration method, counting individuals where they were found on the night of 1 March 1951, supplemented by a special “displaced persons” schedule that recorded place of origin, date of arrival, and current residence. The ISI supplied the punched‑card tabulation machinery — Hollerith machines — that had been used for the 1941 census; these machines were operated by a team of ISI technicians in Delhi and Calcutta. The 1951 census also introduced the concept of “urban agglomeration” to capture the rapid growth of refugee settlements around Delhi, Calcutta, and Bombay.

Data from the 1951 census fed directly into the First Five‑Year Plan (1951‑56), particularly the estimates of labour force, agricultural holdings, and housing deficits. The census revealed that the proportion of the population classified as “displaced persons” was 2.3 % nationally but exceeded 10 % in Punjab and West Bengal. This granularity enabled the Planning Commission to allocate rehabilitation funds on a per‑capita basis rather than through lump‑sum grants. In Pakistan, the 1951 census (held in January 1951) used a similar de‑facto approach but lacked the punched‑card infrastructure; tabulation was performed manually, delaying the release of final tables until 1953. The statistical divergence persisted: India established the Indian Statistical Service (ISS) in 1961, while Pakistan created the Pakistan Statistical Service only in 1973. The 1951 census therefore stands as a landmark not only for its demographic insights but also for the institutional choices it prompted in both successor states.

Comparative Asset Division Analysis
Explain how the partition affected the Indian Meteorological Department's upper‑air observation network and the steps taken to rebuild it within two years.
The India Meteorological Department (IMD) lost its upper‑air stations at Lahore, Peshawar, Quetta, Karachi, and Dhaka because these cities became part of Pakistan (West and East). The remaining Indian stations — Delhi, Pune, Calcutta, and Madras — continued surface observations but lacked radiosonde capability. To restore the network, the IMD procured 12 radiosonde sets from the United Kingdom and the United States under the 1948‑49 technical assistance programme. Indian engineers at the National Physical Laboratory adapted the imported sondes for tropical conditions and established new upper‑air stations at Srinagar, Ahmedabad, Nagpur, and Visakhapatnam by mid‑1949. Simultaneously, the IMD trained 30 observers in a crash course at Pune. By the end of 1949 the department had regained a functional upper‑air network covering the major meteorological zones of the Indian Union, enabling the resumption of regular monsoon forecasting for the 1950 season.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks for “steps taken” after a disruption, structure the answer chronologically: loss → immediate gap → procurement → indigenous adaptation → training → operational restoration. Cite specific years and numbers (e.g., 12 radiosondes, 1949) to demonstrate precision.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Meteorological Department and Upper‑Air Observations

The India Meteorological Department (IMD), established in 1875, operated a network of surface observatories and a fledgling upper‑air programme that relied on radiosonde ascents from a handful of stations. At partition, the IMD lost five upper‑air stations — Lahore, Peshawar, Quetta, Karachi, and Dhaka — which together accounted for roughly 40 % of the pre‑partition radiosonde coverage. The remaining stations at Delhi, Pune, Calcutta, and Madras continued surface observations but could not generate the vertical temperature‑humidity profiles essential for monsoon prediction.

The Government of India responded through a three‑pronged plan executed between late 1947 and December 1949. First, the IMD placed an urgent order for 12 radiosonde kits (Vaisala RS‑11) from the United Kingdom and 8 from the United States under the Technical Cooperation Administration. Second, the National Physical Laboratory (NPL) in Delhi, under the direction of K. S. Krishnan, undertook the calibration and tropicalisation of the sondes, developing a locally manufactured hydrogen‑generation plant to reduce dependence on imported gas cylinders. Third, the IMD launched a six‑month intensive training course at the Pune Meteorological Training Institute for 30 newly recruited observers, covering radiosonde handling, data coding, and transmission via the existing telegraph network.

New upper‑air stations were commissioned at Srinagar (January 1948), Ahmedabad (March 1948), Nagpur (June 1948), and Visakhapatnam (September 1948). By the end of 1949 the network comprised nine operational upper‑air sites, restoring coverage over the Himalayan foothills, the central Indian plateau, and the eastern seaboard. The rebuilt network enabled the IMD to issue its first post‑partition long‑range monsoon forecast for the 1950 season, which was credited with improving agricultural planning in the newly formed states of Punjab, Uttar Pradesh, and West Bengal. In Pakistan, the Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD) was carved out of the IMD’s Lahore and Karachi offices in August 1947; it inherited the remaining radiosonde equipment and, with assistance from the World Meteorological Organization, established its own upper‑air stations at Rawalpindi, Quetta, and Dhaka by 1951. The parallel reconstruction underscores how a scientific service can be split and rebuilt within a short timeframe when political boundaries shift.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Postal Savings Bank and Insurance Schemes

The Post Office Savings Bank (POSB), inaugurated in 1882, and the Postal Life Insurance (PLI) scheme, started in 1884, constituted the largest retail financial network in British India, with over 12 million depositors and 1.3 million policyholders by 1946. Partition required a meticulous division of accounts, reserves, and physical assets. The Partition Council’s Finance Committee, chaired by Sir C. D. Deshmukh, adopted the principle of “account‑holder domicile”: depositors whose registered address fell in the new Pakistani territories were transferred to the newly created Pakistan Post Office Savings Bank (PPOSB), while those in Indian territories remained with the Indian POSB.

The transfer involved the movement of 2.1 million savings accounts and 340,000 insurance policies, representing ₹42 crore in deposits and ₹18 crore in insurance reserves. Records were microfilmed in Calcutta and Lahore; the microfilm reels were exchanged under military escort in October 1947. The Indian POSB absorbed the liabilities of the transferred accounts by crediting the PPOSB with an equivalent amount in sterling balances held at the Bank of England, a settlement finalised in the 1948 Indo‑Pakistan Financial Agreement. The PLI fund, invested largely in government securities, was split proportionally: 78 % remained with India, 22 % moved to Pakistan, reflecting the policyholder distribution.

Post‑partition, the Indian POSB expanded its branch network to serve the refugee population, opening 1,200 new counters in camps around Delhi, Calcutta, and Jammu by 1950. The scheme introduced a “Refugee Savings Certificate” bearing a slightly higher interest rate (3.5 % vs 3 %) to encourage capital formation among displaced persons. In Pakistan, the PPOSB launched a similar “Muhajir Savings Scheme” in 1949. Both institutions retained the original actuarial tables for PLI, but India revised its mortality assumptions in 1952 to reflect the changed demographic profile, while Pakistan adopted the 1949 Indian tables unchanged until its own actuarial review in 1960. The division of the postal savings and insurance apparatus demonstrates how a unified financial inclusion platform can be bifurcated with minimal disruption when legal continuity and clear domicile rules are applied.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Telegraphic and Wireless Communication Networks

The Indian Telegraph Department, governed by the Indian Telegraph Act of 1885, managed a vast network of over 70,000 km of landlines and a growing wireless telegraphy system linking major cities and strategic frontier posts. Partition severed several trunk lines — the Delhi‑Lahore‑Peshawar corridor, the Calcutta‑Dhaka‑Chittagong route, and the Bombay‑Karachi submarine cable — creating immediate communication gaps for both civil administration and military coordination.

The division of assets followed the “geographic continuity” principle agreed upon by the Partition Council’s Communications Sub‑Committee in September 1947. India retained the landline network east of the Radcliffe Line, including the Delhi‑Calcutta, Delhi‑Bombay, and Madras‑Calcutta routes, together with the wireless stations at Delhi, Bombay, Calcutta, Madras, and the high‑frequency (HF) station at Alipore. Pakistan inherited the Lahore, Rawalpindi, Peshawar, Quetta, Karachi, and Dhaka wireless stations, plus the Karachi‑Aden submarine cable. The Bombay‑Aden and Calcutta‑Singapore cables remained with India because their landing points lay in Indian territory.

To restore telegraphic continuity, India undertook a rapid construction programme: a new 1,200 km landline was laid between Delhi and Amritsar (completed March 1948) to replace the severed Delhi‑Lahore link, and a microwave relay chain was commissioned between Delhi and Jammu (operational October 1949) to serve the newly acceded state of Jammu & Kashmir. The wireless service was reorganised into the Indian Wireless Telegraphy Service (IWTS) in 1948, with a central monitoring station at Delhi’s Wireless Monitoring Headquarters. Pakistan established its own Telegraph and Telephone Department (T&T) in Karachi, inheriting the existing staff and equipment; it later signed a bilateral agreement with India in 1950 for the exchange of telegraphic traffic across the border via a dedicated “border circuit” at Wagah.

The partition also accelerated the adoption of teleprinter technology. India imported 150 Creed Model 7 teleprinters in 1948 to replace Morse‑code operators on the main trunk routes, reducing message handling time from hours to minutes. By 1951 the Indian telegraph network carried 1.2 million messages per month, a 30 % increase over the pre‑partition peak, reflecting both the pent‑up demand from refugee communications and the efficiency gains from mechanisation. The experience underscored the strategic importance of resilient communication infrastructure in the immediate aftermath of political division.

  • Assuming the IMD lost all its upper‑air stations; it retained Pune and Delhi and rebuilt the network within two years.
  • Confusing the ISI’s punched‑card machines with later computerisation; the ISI’s mechanical tabulation equipment was used for the 1951 Census, not for the 1961 Census.
  • Believing the CSIR’s laboratories were divided 50:50; the split followed geographic location and staff preference, with a majority of personnel staying in India.
  • Thinking All India Radio’s entire network moved to Pakistan; only the Lahore and Dhaka stations became Radio Pakistan, while the Delhi headquarters and external services remained in India.
  • Assuming the Associated Press of India continued as a single agency; it was bifurcated into the Press Trust of India (India) and the Associated Press of Pakistan (Pakistan).
  • Overlooking that INTUC was formed before Partition (May 1947) as a Congress‑backed alternative to the communist‑led AITUC.
  • Treating the 1947 Industrial Disputes Act as granting a universal right to strike; it only regulated strikes in notified public‑utility services.

The Congress Radio: Underground Broadcasting during the Quit India Movement (1942‑1944)

Origins and Technical Set‑up

The Congress Radio, also known as “Azad Hind Radio” in its early phase, was launched on 27 August 1942 by a group of young Congress volunteers led by Usha Mehta in Bombay. Operating from a concealed attic in the Girgaum area, the station used a 10‑watt short‑wave transmitter assembled from surplus parts. Its frequency was 7.1 MHz (42 metres), chosen to avoid the heavily monitored medium‑wave band. The team broadcast daily for two to three hours, usually between 19:00 and 22:00 IST, transmitting news bulletins, Gandhi’s “Do or Die” message, and updates on parallel governments in Ballia, Tamluk and Satara.

Content and Audience Reach

Programmes combined nationalist speeches, readings from the “Harijan” and “Young India” newspapers, and coded messages for underground workers. The station’s reach extended across the Bombay Presidency, parts of the Central Provinces, and, on clear nights, into the Deccan. Listeners relied on homemade crystal sets; the British estimated a regular audience of 30,000‑50,000. The broadcasts were a morale lifeline after the arrest of the entire Congress Working Committee on 9 August 1942.

British Counter‑Measures

The Government of India deployed direction‑finding vans, jammed the frequency with a 5‑kW transmitter at the Bombay Radio Station, and launched a massive manhunt. On 12 November 1942 the police raided the attic, arresting Usha Mehta, Vithalbhai Jhaveri and others. The transmitter was seized, but a backup unit hidden in a nearby chawl allowed sporadic transmissions until March 1943. The British also used the Defence of India Act to prosecute the operators, sentencing Mehta to four years’ rigorous imprisonment.

Historical Significance

Though short‑lived, the Congress Radio demonstrated the capacity of nationalist networks to create an alternative information infrastructure under total censorship. It pre‑figured the later “Azad Hind Radio” run by Subhas Chandra Bose from Singapore and showed that non‑violent resistance could adopt modern technology. Historians regard it as a rare instance of mass‑media subversion in a colonial context, complementing the parallel governments documented elsewhere in this lesson.

The Forward Bloc and the Indian National Army: Subhas Chandra Bose’s Parallel Nationalist Project (1939‑1945)

Formation of the Forward Bloc

After resigning as Congress President in 1939, Subhas Chandra Bose founded the Forward Bloc on 22 June 1939 as a left‑wing faction within the Congress. Its programme demanded complete independence, a planned economy, and the mobilisation of peasants and workers. The Bloc attracted radical elements from Bengal, Punjab and the United Provinces, but was expelled from the Congress in August 1939 for “anti‑party activities”.

Bose’s Escape and the Azad Hind Government

In January 1941 Bose escaped house arrest in Calcutta, travelling via Afghanistan and the Soviet Union to Berlin. There he secured German support for a “Free India Centre” and began recruiting Indian prisoners of war. In 1943 he moved to Japanese‑occupied Southeast Asia, where he assumed leadership of the Indian National Army (INA), originally formed by Mohan Singh in 1942. On 21 October 1943 Bose proclaimed the Provisional Government of Azad Hind in Singapore, recognised by Japan, Germany, Italy and several other Axis states.

Military Campaigns: Imphal and Kohima

The INA’s 1st and 2nd Divisions, totalling roughly 40,000 troops, participated in the Japanese U‑Go offensive against Imphal and Kohima (March‑July 1944). Though the offensive failed, the INA’s presence forced the British to divert troops from the Burma front and heightened anxiety about the loyalty of the Indian Army. The subsequent retreat through the Malay Peninsula saw heavy casualties; many INA soldiers surrendered to British forces in 1945.

Political Impact on the Nationalist Movement

The INA trials at the Red Fort (November 1945‑January 1946) turned the defeated soldiers into martyrs. Massive public demonstrations, supported by the Congress, the Muslim League and the Communist Party, pressured the British to commute death sentences. The episode demonstrated that a militarised nationalist alternative could galvanise public opinion even without territorial success, influencing the British decision to negotiate transfer of power.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “The Forward Bloc was formed as a faction within the Indian National Congress in 1939. Which of the following statements about its early programme is correct? (a) It advocated dominion status within the British Empire. (b) It demanded complete independence and a planned economy. (c) It supported the British war effort unconditionally. (d) It opposed any alliance with Axis powers.”
The correct answer is (b). The Forward Bloc’s 1939 manifesto explicitly called for “complete independence” and a “planned economy” based on socialist principles. It did not seek dominion status, nor did it support the British war effort; Bose later sought Axis assistance, but the Bloc’s original programme was anti‑imperialist and left‑oriented.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links an organisation to a specific year, first verify the founding date and the immediate political context. For the Forward Bloc, remember the 1939 split from Congress and the 1941 escape; these two dates anchor most factual MCQs. Write the key phrase “complete independence + planned economy” in your margin notes for quick recall.

The Hindu Mahasabha, the RSS and the Politics of Partition (1937‑1947)

Ideological Foundations

The Hindu Mahasabha, revived under V. D. Savarkar in 1937, propagated “Hindutva” as a cultural‑nationalist ideology distinct from the Congress’s territorial nationalism. The Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh (RSS), founded by K. B. Hedgewar in 1925, expanded rapidly in the 1930s through its shakha network, emphasizing discipline, martial training and a vision of a Hindu Rashtra. Both organisations viewed the Muslim League’s demand for Pakistan as a threat to Hindu unity.

Electoral and Mobilisational Activities

In the 1937 provincial elections the Mahasabha contested seats in the Central Provinces, Bombay and the United Provinces, winning a modest number but establishing a parliamentary presence. The RSS, though officially apolitical, encouraged its swayamsevaks to vote for “Hindu‑friendly” candidates. During the 1942 Quit India Movement the Mahasabha officially opposed the Congress call, arguing that a mass uprising would weaken Hindu defence against a possible Muslim‑League‑led partition.

Role in the 1946‑47 Negotiations

Savarkar’s “two‑nation theory” rebuttal — “Hindus and Muslims are two nations but one country” — was cited in the 1946 Cabinet Mission discussions. The Mahasabha lobbied the British for a strong centre and safeguards for Hindu minorities in the proposed provinces. The RSS, while not participating directly, organised relief camps for Hindu refugees from Punjab and Bengal, thereby shaping the humanitarian narrative that influenced the Mountbatten Plan’s boundary decisions.

Post‑Partition Legacy

After 15 August 1947 the Mahasabha’s political relevance declined, but the RSS grew into a mass organisation that later spawned the Bharatiya Jana Sangh (1951) and the Bharatiya Janata Party (1980). The partition violence reinforced the RSS’s narrative of Hindu vulnerability, a theme that continues to inform Indian electoral politics.

The 1942‑43 Burma Campaign and the INA’s Military Operations: Strategic Impact on British War Planning

Japanese Advance and the INA’s Deployment

Following the fall of Rangoon in March 1942, the Japanese 15th Army pushed into the Arakan and central Burma. The INA’s 1st Division, under Colonel Prem Kumar Sahgal, was attached to the Japanese 33rd Division for the Arakan offensive (January‑February 1943). Though the offensive stalled, the INA gained combat experience and propaganda value.

Imphal‑Kohima Offensive (U‑Go) 1944

The decisive engagement came with Operation U‑Go (March‑July 1944). The INA’s 2nd Division, commanded by Colonel Gurbaksh Singh Dhillon, fought alongside the Japanese 15th and 31st Divisions against the British 14th Army under General Slim. The INA’s role was primarily in the southern flank near the Kabaw Valley. Heavy monsoon rains, Allied air superiority and logistical collapse forced a Japanese‑INA retreat. British casualties were approximately 12,500; INA losses exceeded 8,000.

British Strategic Re‑assessment

The presence of Indian troops fighting under a nationalist banner alarmed the British High Command. Field Marshal Wavell reported to London that the INA’s existence undermined the “martial races” recruitment policy and could trigger mutinies in the Indian Army. Consequently, the British accelerated plans for the 1945‑46 elections and the Cabinet Mission, hoping to pre‑empt a post‑war nationalist upsurge.

Post‑War Historiography

Indian nationalist historians argue that the INA’s military failure was outweighed by its political success: the Red Fort trials turned the defeated soldiers into symbols of resistance. British official histories, however, emphasise the operational irrelevance of the INA, noting that the 14th Army’s victory at Imphal‑Kohima was achieved without decisive INA contribution. Both perspectives are required for a balanced answer in the examination.

The 1947 Industrial Disputes Act: Legislative Design, Scope and Immediate Impact

Background and Enactment

The Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (Act 14 of 1947) was passed by the Constituent Assembly (Legislative) on 11 March 1947 and received the Governor‑General’s assent on 31 March 1947. It replaced the Trade Disputes Act, 1929, and aimed to provide a comprehensive machinery for the investigation and settlement of industrial disputes in British India, continuing in force after independence under Article 372 of the Constitution.

Key Provisions

  • Definition of “industry”: Any systematic activity carried on by cooperation between employer and workmen for the production, supply or distribution of goods or services (Section 2(j)).
  • Works Committees: Mandatory in establishments employing 100 or more workmen (Section 3).
  • Conciliation Officers and Boards: Appointed by the appropriate government; compulsory conciliation before a strike or lock‑out in public‑utility services (Section 4‑5).
  • Labour Courts, Industrial Tribunals and National Tribunals: Three‑tier adjudication hierarchy (Sections 7‑10).
  • Strike and Lock‑out Restrictions: Prohibited in public‑utility services without 14‑day notice and during conciliation/adjudication proceedings (Section 22‑23).
  • Lay‑off, Retrenchment and Closure: Compensation and prior permission requirements for establishments of 50+ workmen (Sections 25‑25K).

Immediate Impact (1947‑1950)

The Act institutionalised tripartite dialogue at a time when the Interim Government faced a wave of strikes in textiles (Bombay, Ahmedabad), railways and coal. The mandatory conciliation stage reduced the frequency of wildcat strikes, but the narrow definition of “public‑utility service” limited the Act’s reach. The 1948 amendment extended the notice period to six weeks for essential services. The legislation laid the groundwork for the 1956 Industrial Disputes (Amendment) Act, which introduced the “unfair labour practice” schedule.

Continuities and Critiques

Scholars note that the Act retained the colonial emphasis on industrial peace over workers’ rights, reflected in the restrictive strike provisions. However, the creation of Labour Courts provided a legal forum absent under the 1929 Act. The Act’s survival into the post‑colonial period illustrates the continuity of labour‑law architecture across the transfer of power.

  • Confusing the Congress Radio’s 1942 launch with the later Azad Hind Radio operated from Singapore; they were distinct operations.
  • Attributing the Forward Bloc’s 1939 programme to the 1943 Azad Hind Government; the Bloc’s socialist agenda predated Bose’s Axis alliance.
  • Assuming the Hindu Mahasabha and the RSS acted as a single electoral bloc in 1946; the RSS remained officially non‑political until the 1950s.
  • Overstating the INA’s combat effectiveness at Imphal‑Kohima; British records show the INA’s role was auxiliary and its numbers were inflated in nationalist narratives.
  • Believing the 1947 Industrial Disputes Act granted a universal right to strike; it only regulated strikes in notified public‑utility services.
  • Neglecting the 1948 amendment that extended the strike notice period to six weeks for essential services.
  • Treating the 1947 Act as a wholly new creation; it retained the conciliation‑adjudication framework of the 1929 Trade Disputes Act with expanded coverage.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Postal Savings Bank and Post Office Life Insurance Fund

The Indian Postal Savings Bank (POSB), established under the Post Office Savings Bank Act of 1881, operated an extensive network of post offices holding substantial deposits by 1947. The Post Office Life Insurance Fund (PLI), launched in 1884, provided low‑premium life cover to government employees and the public with a significant policy portfolio. When the Partition Council met in mid‑1947, a dedicated Postal Committee was tasked with dividing assets, liabilities, and personnel on a territorial basis. The committee applied a “geographical continuity” principle: post offices and their records in the provinces awarded to Pakistan were transferred to the newly created Pakistan Post Office Savings Bank, while the remainder stayed with the Indian POSB.

The division required a physical handover of ledger books, deposit certificates, and insurance policy registers. To avoid disruption, the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) acted as the interim clearing house: balances were settled at the pre‑partition exchange rate, and the RBI issued a special adjustment account to reconcile differences. Staff opted for either dominion; a large number of postal employees moved to Pakistan, while the rest remained in India. The Indian POSB continued under the same statutory framework, but the Post Office Savings Bank (Amendment) Act, 1948, updated the definition of “post office” to reflect the new territorial limits. Pakistan enacted its own Post Office Savings Bank Ordinance in 1948, mirroring the Indian legislation but with a separate board of management.

Key outcomes included the preservation of depositor confidence — no depositor lost principal — and the establishment of two independent savings institutions that later evolved into national savings bodies in both countries. The PLI portfolio was split proportionally; policyholders in Pakistan received new policy numbers issued by the Pakistan Post Office Life Insurance Fund, while Indian policyholders retained their original numbers. The exercise demonstrated how a colonial financial infrastructure could be partitioned with minimal systemic shock, a precedent cited during the later bifurcation of Bangladesh’s postal savings system.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Telegraph and Wireless Services

The Indian Telegraph Department, governed by the Indian Telegraph Act of 1885, managed a vast network of landlines, telegraph offices, and high‑frequency wireless stations linking major cities. The Partition Council’s Telegraph Committee applied a “line‑by‑line” allocation: each telegraph circuit was assigned to the dominion in which its terminating offices lay. Where a line crossed the new border — for example the Delhi‑Lahore trunk — the committee ordered a physical cut at the border and the installation of duplicate terminal equipment on each side.

Wireless stations posed a special challenge because they served both dominions. The high‑power station at Karachi was allocated to Pakistan, while the Delhi station remained with India. The committee arranged a joint operating agreement for the shared frequency band, valid for a transitional period, after which each dominion secured its own International Telecommunication Union (ITU) allocations. Personnel transfers followed the same voluntary‑option principle used for the postal service; a significant number of telegraphists and engineers opted for Pakistan, the rest stayed in India. The Indian Telegraph Department was renamed the Department of Telecommunications in 1950, while Pakistan established the Pakistan Telegraph and Telephone Department under the Pakistan Telegraph Act, 1948.

Asset valuation was based on the Telegraph Department’s capital account. The Partition Council approved a split reflecting the larger mileage and office count on the Indian side. The settlement was effected through the RBI’s adjustment account, with payments made in rupees convertible at a fixed rate until the 1949 devaluation. The reorganisation ensured continuity of international telegraph traffic — vital for diplomatic and commercial communications — and laid the groundwork for the separate national telecom policies that emerged in the 1950s.

Technique: Committee‑Based Asset Allocation
Explain how the Partition Council’s Telegraph Committee allocated the Delhi‑Lahore trunk line and the Karachi wireless station, and why a joint frequency agreement was necessary.
The committee assigned each telegraph circuit to the dominion where its terminating offices were located. The Delhi‑Lahore trunk crossed the new border, so it was physically severed at the boundary and duplicate terminal equipment installed on both sides. The Karachi high‑power wireless station fell in Pakistani territory and was allocated to Pakistan, while the Delhi station stayed with India. Because both stations had previously shared a frequency band for inter‑dominion traffic, a temporary joint operating agreement was signed to avoid interference until each dominion obtained its own ITU frequency allocations.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the partition of a colonial department, identify the specific Partition Council committee (Postal, Telegraph, Meteorological, Survey, Agricultural). Remember the three‑step pattern: (1) territorial principle for asset allocation, (2) voluntary staff option, (3) financial settlement via the RBI’s adjustment accounts. This pattern repeats across all five reorganisation topics and earns full marks if cited correctly.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Meteorological Department

The Indian Meteorological Department (IMD), founded in 1875, operated a network of surface observatories, upper‑air stations, and a central forecasting office in Poona (now Pune). The Partition Council’s Meteorological Committee divided the observatories on a strict territorial basis: stations in the provinces awarded to Pakistan were transferred to the newly created Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD). The remaining stations stayed with the IMD.

Data continuity was a priority. The committee mandated that all historical climate records up to the partition date be duplicated; one set remained in India, the other was shipped to Karachi. Instruments such as barometers, anemometers, and radiosonde equipment were allocated proportionally. The IMD’s upper‑air station at Srinagar, though located in the princely state of Jammu & Kashmir, was retained by India because the state’s accession occurred before the committee’s final report. Pakistan established its own upper‑air station at Karachi using equipment transferred from the IMD’s Karachi observatory.

Financial settlement followed the standard RBI adjustment account mechanism. The IMD’s capital expenditure was split in India’s favour, reflecting the larger number of stations. The PMD adopted the IMD’s forecasting codes and the “Indian Daily Weather Report” format, renaming it the “Pakistan Daily Weather Report” from early 1948. Both departments continued to exchange synoptic data under a bilateral agreement signed shortly after partition, a rare instance of sustained scientific cooperation amid political hostility.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Survey of India

The Survey of India, the premier mapping agency since the 18th century, held the national geodetic framework — the Great Trigonometrical Survey (GTS) triangulation network — and a vast repository of topographic sheets at various scales. The Partition Council’s Survey Committee applied a “sheet‑by‑sheet” division: each published map sheet covering territory assigned to Pakistan was transferred to the newly formed Survey of Pakistan (SoP). Sheets straddling the border were split along the Radcliffe Line, requiring new engraving of border symbology.

Field parties and survey instruments were allocated on a personnel‑option basis. A substantial number of surveyors, draftsmen, and instrument technicians opted for Pakistan; the remainder stayed with the Survey of India. The GTS control points lying in Pakistani territory were handed over with their coordinate values, enabling the SoP to continue the national triangulation without re‑observation. The Survey of India retained the central computing office at Dehra Dun, while the SoP established its computing centre at Rawalpindi.

Financial settlement valued the Survey’s assets, and the Partition Council approved a split favouring India, reflecting the larger map‑sheet inventory. The RBI’s adjustment account effected the transfer. Both organisations agreed to exchange revised map sheets annually for a transitional period, after which each maintained independent cartographic programmes. The division set a precedent for the later creation of the Survey of Bangladesh, which inherited the East Pakistan sheet series.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Council of Agricultural Research

The Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR), established in 1929 as the Imperial Council of Agricultural Research, coordinated a network of central research institutes — including the Indian Agricultural Research Institute (IARI) at Pusa, the Indian Veterinary Research Institute (IVRI) at Izatnagar, and the National Dairy Research Institute (NDRI) at Karnal — plus numerous regional stations. The Partition Council’s Agricultural Committee divided institutes on a location basis: institutes in areas remaining with India stayed under ICAR; those in areas awarded to Pakistan were transferred.

Research staff exercised the voluntary option; a significant number of scientists and technical officers moved to Pakistan, forming the nucleus of the Pakistan Agricultural Research Council (PARC), formally constituted in 1948. Germplasm collections — wheat, cotton, rice, and livestock breeds — were duplicated where possible; unique lines held at institutes in Pakistan were transferred there, while collections in India remained. The committee arranged a joint “Germplasm Exchange Protocol” for a transitional period, after which each council managed its own gene banks.

The ICAR’s budget was split in India’s favour, reflecting the larger number of institutes and staff. The RBI’s adjustment account handled the monetary transfer. Both councils retained a shared publication for a brief period, after which Pakistan launched its own journal. The reorganisation ensured that agricultural research capacity — critical for food security in both nascent states — was preserved despite the abrupt political division.

  • Confusing the Postal Committee’s “geographical continuity” rule with the Telegraph Committee’s “line‑by‑line” rule; they are distinct allocation principles.
  • Assuming the Karachi wireless station remained under joint control after the transitional period; the joint frequency agreement expired once separate ITU allocations were secured.
  • Overlooking the voluntary‑option principle for staff transfers, which applied uniformly across postal, telegraph, meteorological, survey, and agricultural services.
  • Misstating the financial split ratios; each department had its own ratio based on asset inventory, not a uniform 50:50.
  • Neglecting the role of the Reserve Bank’s adjustment accounts as the primary settlement mechanism for all five reorganisations.
  • Believing the Survey of India’s Great Trigonometrical Survey control points were re‑observed after partition; they were transferred with coordinates intact.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Statistical Service and Census Operations

The Partition Council set up a Statistical Committee in mid‑1947 to divide the statistical machinery of British India. The committee applied a location‑based principle for physical assets: the Indian Statistical Institute (ISI) at Calcutta, the Central Statistical Office (CSO) in New Delhi, and the 1941 Census library remained with India; statistical units in Lahore, Karachi and Dhaka formed the nucleus of Pakistan’s Central Statistical Office (established 1950).

Personnel transfers followed the voluntary‑option rule common to all services. Officers of the Indian Statistical Service and subordinate staff could opt for either dominion; a significant number of Muslim statisticians chose Pakistan, while the majority stayed in India. The committee arranged a one‑time duplication of the 1941 Census schedules so both dominions held complete enumeration records for revenue‑sharing calculations. Financial settlement of statistical assets was effected through the Reserve Bank of India’s adjustment account, weighted toward India reflecting the larger volume of records and the ISI’s research infrastructure.

The immediate operational consequence was separate decennial censuses in 1951 (India, West Pakistan, East Pakistan). Both organisations used the 1941 questionnaire as a baseline but added new categories — refugee status in India, “Muhajir” identity in Pakistan — to capture demographic upheaval. The Indian Statistical Service was formally constituted as an organised Group A service in 1961; Pakistan created the Pakistan Statistical Service in 1952. The preserved 1941 data series enabled comparable time‑series for planning and subsequent Five‑Year Plans.

Key Allocation Mechanisms

  • Location‑based asset division for institutes and offices.
  • Voluntary option for statistical personnel.
  • Duplication of the 1941 Census schedules for both dominions.
  • Financial settlement via RBI adjustment account, weighted by asset inventory.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Forest Service and Environmental Governance

The Partition Council’s Forest Committee, chaired by the Inspector‑General of Forests, met in August 1947 to divide the imperial forest estate. The guiding principle was territorial continuity: forest blocks, working plans, and research stations on the Indian side of the Radcliffe Line were retained by India; those on the Pakistani side were transferred. The Forest Research Institute (FRI) and the Imperial Forest College (later Indian Forest College) at Dehradun, and the Central Academy for State Forest Service remained in India. Pakistan established the Pakistan Forest Institute at Peshawar (1948) and the Pakistan Forest College at Abbottabad (1950).

Officers of the Imperial Forest Service (IFS) exercised the voluntary option. The split roughly mirrored the forest‑area division (approximately 70 % of reserved forest area remained in India). The committee also divided working‑plan records, silvicultural research data, and herbarium collections. Unique specimens at the FRI were duplicated where possible; type specimens of species endemic to the western Himalaya stayed in India, while those from the Punjab plains and Sindh were handed to Pakistan.

Financial settlement followed the standard RBI adjustment‑account mechanism, weighted toward India because of the larger forest‑area inventory, FRI’s laboratory infrastructure, and timber‑revenue streams from the Terai and Central Indian reserves. The partition necessitated re‑defining forest boundaries along the new international border; joint demarcation teams surveyed the line in 1948‑49, and the resulting boundary maps were exchanged to prevent disputes over illicit felling. The Indian Forest Service was reconstituted as an All‑India Service in 1966 under the All‑India Services Act, 1951; Pakistan’s forest cadre remained a provincial service until the creation of the Pakistan Forest Service in 1973.

Division Highlights

  • Territorial continuity for forest blocks and research institutes.
  • Voluntary option for Imperial Forest Service officers.
  • Duplication of herbarium type specimens where feasible.
  • RBI adjustment‑account settlement weighted by forest‑area and revenue.
  • Joint demarcation of the international border in 1948‑49.
Committee‑Based Asset Division Technique
Explain how the Partition Council’s Forest Committee ensured continuity of forest research and revenue collection after August 1947.
The Forest Committee applied a territorial‑continuity rule: all forest blocks, working‑plan records, and research institutes located on the Indian side of the Radcliffe Line (notably the Forest Research Institute and the Imperial Forest College at Dehradun) were retained by India, while assets in the western Punjab, Sindh, and the North‑West Frontier Province were transferred to Pakistan. Officers of the Imperial Forest Service exercised a voluntary option, resulting in a split that mirrored the forest‑area ratio. The committee arranged duplication of herbarium type specimens and silvicultural data so both dominions possessed baseline research material. Revenue‑generating timber reserves were divided by area, and the financial settlement was processed through the RBI adjustment account, with India receiving a larger share reflecting its greater forest‑area inventory. Joint demarcation teams surveyed the new international boundary in 1948‑49, producing exchanged maps that prevented cross‑border timber theft. This combination of territorial allocation, personnel option, data duplication, and financial adjustment ensured that both India and Pakistan could continue forest management, research, and revenue collection without interruption.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks about the reorganisation of a specific service at partition, identify the three standard mechanisms used by the Partition Council: (1) territorial continuity for physical assets, (2) voluntary option for personnel, and (3) RBI adjustment‑account settlement weighted by asset inventory. Cite the relevant committee (Statistical, Forest, Judicial, Audit) and note any unique feature — e.g., duplication of census schedules for the Statistical Committee, or joint demarcation of the international border for forests. This framework lets you answer any service‑reorganisation question with precision and speed.

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Judicial Service and High Courts

The Partition Council’s Judicial Committee, convened in September 1947, oversaw the division of the Federal Court of India (established 1937) and the provincial High Courts. The Federal Court’s records, library, and pending appeals were split: cases arising from territories allocated to Pakistan were transferred to the newly created Federal Court of Pakistan (inaugurated 1948), while the remainder stayed with the Federal Court of India, which became the Supreme Court of India on 26 January 1950. The High Courts of Lahore, Dhaka, and Sindh became the High Courts of West Punjab, East Bengal, and Sindh respectively in Pakistan; the High Courts of Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, Allahabad, Patna, and Nagpur continued in India.

Judges exercised the voluntary option. At the Federal Court level, the majority opted for India; at the High Court level, the option was exercised individually — the Lahore High Court lost several Hindu judges who chose India, while the Calcutta High Court saw a few Muslim judges move to Pakistan. The committee arranged for the physical transfer of case files, judgment registers, and the Indian Law Reports series. Where a case involved parties from both dominions, a special protocol allowed the originating court to retain jurisdiction until final disposal, after which the record was transferred.

Financial settlement of judicial assets — court buildings, libraries, and the Federal Court’s endowment — was handled through the RBI adjustment account. India received a larger share because the Supreme Court building in New Delhi and the bulk of the law‑report publishing infrastructure remained in Indian territory. The committee also agreed on a transitional arrangement for the enforcement of decrees across the new border: a reciprocal recognition protocol, valid until 1950, allowed decrees of Indian High Courts to be executed in Pakistan and vice‑versa. This protocol was later superseded by the 1950 India‑Pakistan Agreement on Enforcement of Civil Court Decrees.

Judicial Division Summary

  • Federal Court records split by territorial jurisdiction of appeals.
  • High Courts reassigned according to the Radcliffe Line.
  • Voluntary option for judges; majority opted for India at Federal Court level.
  • Special protocol for cross‑dominion pending cases.
  • RBI adjustment‑account settlement weighted toward India.
  • Reciprocal decree‑enforcement protocol (1947‑1950).

The 1947 Partition and the Reorganisation of the Indian Audit and Accounts Service

The Partition Council’s Audit Committee, chaired by the Auditor General of India, met in October 1947 to divide the Indian Audit and Accounts Department (IA&AD). The department’s headquarters in New Delhi, the Central Revenues Audit Office, the Railway Audit Directorate, and the Defence Audit Wing remained with India. Pakistan received the Audit Offices in Lahore, Karachi, and Peshawar, which formed the nucleus of the Office of the Auditor General of Pakistan, formally established in 1948.

Personnel transfers followed the voluntary‑option principle. The majority of IA&AD officers and staff opted for India; a smaller proportion chose Pakistan. The committee ensured each audit wing retained a core of experienced officers; for instance, the Defence Audit Wing kept its senior Indian officers, while the newly created Pakistan Defence Audit Wing was staffed by officers who opted for Pakistan and supplemented by transfers from the Military Accounts Department.

The division of audit records was based on the territorial origin of the accounts. Central revenue accounts for provinces that became part of Pakistan were handed over, together with the corresponding appropriation accounts and audit reports. The committee arranged a one‑time duplication of the consolidated Appropriation Accounts for 1946‑47 so that both dominions could close their first post‑partition financial year. Financial settlement of the department’s assets — buildings, furniture, and the audit‑report printing press — was effected through the RBI adjustment account, with India receiving a larger share reflecting the greater volume of central revenue and railway audit work.

The Indian Audit and Accounts Service (IA&AS) was formally constituted as a Group A civil service in 1950 under the All‑India Services Act, 1951. Pakistan’s audit cadre remained a central service until the creation of the Pakistan Audit and Accounts Service in 1973. The 1947 division established a precedent for the subsequent bifurcation of the Comptroller and Auditor General’s office during the 1971 creation of Bangladesh, where the same principles of territorial continuity, voluntary option, and RBI‑mediated settlement were reapplied.

Audit Division Mechanics

  • Territorial allocation of audit offices and directorates.
  • Voluntary option for IA&AD officers and staff (majority opted for India).
  • Duplication of 1946‑47 Appropriation Accounts for both dominions.
  • RBI adjustment‑account settlement weighted by audit workload.
  • Precedent for 1971 Bangladesh audit reorganisation.
  • Confusing the Statistical Committee’s duplication of census schedules with the Forest Committee’s herbarium duplication; they served different record‑types.
  • Assuming the Federal Court of Pakistan was created before the Federal Court of India became the Supreme Court; the Federal Court of Pakistan was inaugurated in 1948, the Supreme Court of India in 1950.
  • Overlooking the voluntary‑option principle for judges and audit officers, which was uniform across all services.
  • Misstating the financial settlement mechanism; every service used the RBI adjustment account, not a direct cash transfer.
  • Believing the Indian Forest Service was an All‑India Service at partition; it was reconstituted as such only in 1966.
  • Neglecting the reciprocal decree‑enforcement protocol that operated between Indian and Pakistani High Courts from 1947 to 1950.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise factual recall when comparing the reorganisation mechanisms of different services at partition.

The 1931 Gandhi‑Irwin Pact: Negotiations, Terms, and Political Consequences

The Gandhi‑Irwin Pact, signed on 5 March 1931, marked the first formal agreement between the Indian National Congress and the British government after the launch of the Civil Disobedience Movement. Viceroy Lord Irwin, seeking to defuse escalating unrest and secure Congress participation in the Second Round Table Conference in London, released Mahatma Gandhi and other leaders from detention in January 1931. The negotiations, conducted over several weeks at the Viceroy’s House in Delhi, centred on three core demands: the release of all political prisoners arrested during the salt satyagraha, the restoration of confiscated property and licences, and the right to peaceful picketing of liquor and foreign‑cloth shops.

The final text conceded the release of prisoners not charged with violence, the return of fines and confiscated goods, and permission for non‑violent picketing. However, the British refused to repeal the salt tax, to grant a full amnesty for those convicted of violent offences, or to concede the demand for responsible government at the centre. Gandhi accepted the pact on the grounds that it secured a breathing space for the movement and demonstrated the efficacy of non‑violent negotiation. The Congress Working Committee ratified the agreement on 7 March 1931, and Gandhi sailed for London to attend the Second Round Table Conference in September.

Politically, the pact produced mixed outcomes. It enabled Congress to claim a moral victory and present a united front at the conference, but the British used the conference to emphasise communal safeguards and sideline the demand for full independence. Within Congress, the pact sparked sharp criticism from left‑wing leaders such as Subhas Chandra Bose and Jawaharlal Nehru, who viewed the concessions as insufficient and a betrayal of the “Purna Swaraj” resolution of 1929. The pact also led the British to rely more heavily on existing security legislation, anticipating a renewed mass movement. Historians regard the Gandhi‑Irwin Pact as a tactical pause that revealed both the potential and the limits of negotiated settlement under colonial rule.

The 1935 Government of India Act’s Federal Scheme and the Princely States’ Response

The Government of India Act 1935 introduced a federal structure that envisaged a union of British Indian provinces and princely states under a central legislature with dyarchy at the centre. The Act provided for a Federal Assembly and a Council of State, with representation for princely states based on a formula that weighted population and revenue. Crucially, accession to the federation was voluntary; each ruler had to sign an Instrument of Accession accepting federal authority over defence, external affairs, communications, and ancillary subjects. The Chamber of Princes, reconstituted as a consultative body, was meant to articulate the states’ collective interests.

In practice, the response of the princely states was fragmented. Larger states such as Mysore, Travancore, and Baroda negotiated favourable terms, securing guarantees for their internal autonomy and financial privileges. Hyderabad, the largest state, initially resisted accession, demanding a separate treaty that would preserve its sovereign status. The Nizam’s government also explored possible alliances with other Muslim‑majority states. Smaller states, lacking administrative capacity, were more inclined to accede, hoping for British protection against internal unrest. The outbreak of the Second World War in 1939 and the subsequent resignation of Congress ministries in 1939‑40 stalled the federal scheme, as the Viceroy invoked emergency powers and the federation never came into operation.

The Act’s federal provisions nevertheless shaped post‑war negotiations. The 1946 Cabinet Mission Plan revived the idea of a three‑tier federation, grouping provinces and states, but the princely states’ reluctance to surrender paramountcy became a critical obstacle. The eventual integration of the states after 1947 relied on the Instrument of Accession framework established by the 1935 Act, supplemented by the States’ Ministry under Sardar Patel. The experience demonstrated that voluntary accession, while legally elegant, proved politically unstable when the central authority lacked coercive power.

Examiner's technique: Comparative constitutional analysis
Compare the federal scheme of the 1935 Act with the Cabinet Mission’s three‑tier proposal. Identify two structural similarities and one fundamental difference in the treatment of princely states.
Similarities: both envisaged a federal union with a central legislature and allowed princely states to join voluntarily through an Instrument of Accession; both allocated representation to states on a weighted basis. Difference: the 1935 Act required individual accession to a single federation, whereas the Cabinet Mission proposed grouping of provinces and states into three sections with optional participation, giving states a collective bargaining platform absent in the 1935 scheme.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for “compare and contrast”, structure the answer in a two‑column table or bullet pairs. Explicitly label each point as “Similarity” or “Difference” to make the examiner’s marking easy.

The Quit India Movement’s Impact on British Military Recruitment and the Indian Army’s Loyalty (1942‑44)

The Quit India Movement, launched on 8 August 1942, coincided with the British Empire’s most critical manpower crisis in the Second World War. The Indian Army, which had expanded from roughly 200,000 men in 1939 to over 2.5 million by 1945, depended on a steady flow of volunteers from the peasantry of Punjab, the North‑West Frontier, and the Madras Presidency. The mass arrests of Congress leaders, the use of the Defence of India Act, and widespread civil disobedience disrupted traditional recruiting networks run by district officials and zamindars. Recruitment rallies were cancelled, and the propaganda of “Do or Die” discouraged enlistment by framing the war as a British imperial venture.

British military authorities responded by intensifying recruitment drives in areas less affected by the movement, notably the Sikh and Gurkha recruiting grounds, and by offering enhanced pay, land grants, and post‑war pensions. The Commander‑in‑Chief, General Auchinleck, issued directives emphasising the army’s apolitical character and warning against political sympathy. Despite these measures, contemporary reports indicate a dip in voluntary enlistments during late 1942 and early 1943, particularly in the United Provinces and Bihar.

The loyalty of the Indian Army was tested further by the formation of the Indian National Army (INA) under Subhas Chandra Bose, which recruited prisoners of war captured in Malaya and Burma. The British countered with propaganda campaigns highlighting the INA’s collaboration with Japan and stressing the army’s role in defending India’s borders. The 1943‑44 Bengal famine, exacerbated by wartime logistics, also strained civil‑military relations, as troops were deployed for internal security rather than front‑line duties. By 1944, the recruitment shortfall had been largely offset by expanded recruitment drives and the deployment of non‑combatant labour corps, but the episode revealed the fragility of colonial military manpower when nationalist sentiment intersected with wartime exigencies.

The Interplay Between the INA Trials and the 1946 Royal Indian Navy Mutiny

The Red Fort trials of three INA officers — Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Sahgal, and Gurbaksh Singh Dhillon — opened in November 1945 and concluded in May 1946. The trials, conducted under the Indian Army Act, charged the defendants with waging war against the King‑Emperor. The defence, led by Bhulabhai Desai, argued that the INA was a legitimate liberation army and that the accused were entitled to the protections of international law. The proceedings attracted massive public attention; daily newspaper reports, mass meetings organised by the Congress, the Muslim League, and the Communist Party turned the courtroom into a nationalist theatre.

The emotional resonance of the trials permeated the Royal Indian Navy (RIN). In February 1946, ratings aboard HMIS Talwar in Bombay harbour, incensed by racial discrimination, poor food, and the perceived injustice of the INA convictions, initiated a strike that rapidly spread to 78 ships and 20 shore establishments, involving over 20,000 ratings. The mutineers hoisted the tricolour alongside the Muslim League flag, demanded the release of INA prisoners, and called for the withdrawal of British troops from Indonesia. The British responded with a combination of negotiations and force: Admiral Godfrey offered concessions on pay and conditions, while Royal Marines and the Army surrounded the naval bases. The mutiny collapsed within a week after the Congress and the Muslim League, fearing a breakdown of order, urged the ratings to surrender.

The linkage between the INA trials and the RIN mutiny is evident in the mutineers’ charter, which explicitly referenced the “INA heroes” and demanded their unconditional release. British assessments noted that the trials had radicalised a segment of the armed forces that had hitherto been considered reliable. The episode accelerated the British decision to transfer power, as the loyalty of the Indian armed forces could no longer be taken for granted. In the subsequent Cabinet Mission negotiations, the British insisted on a phased withdrawal of troops, citing the mutiny as evidence that the imperial garrison was no longer a dependable instrument of control.

The 1947 Partition’s Reorganisation of the Indian Meteorological Department and Scientific Services

The Indian Meteorological Department (IMD), established in 1875, operated a network of observatories, seismological stations, and upper‑air stations across the subcontinent. At partition, the Radcliffe Line divided this infrastructure: key observatories at Lahore, Peshawar, and Karachi fell to Pakistan, while the headquarters at Simla (later New Delhi), the Calcutta observatory, and the Poona upper‑air station remained with India. The Partition Council’s Scientific Committee, chaired by the Director‑General of Observatories, met in September 1947 to allocate assets, personnel, and data archives.

Personnel transfers followed the voluntary‑option principle. The majority of IMD staff opted for India, with a smaller number choosing Pakistan. The committee arranged for the duplication of climatological records covering the period 1901‑1947 so that both dominions could maintain continuous weather series. Instrumental assets — barometers, anemometers, and radiosonde equipment — were divided on a territorial basis, with the Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD) receiving the Lahore and Karachi upper‑air units. A joint committee continued to exchange synoptic charts for some years before the two departments established independent telegraphic circuits.

The reorganisation also affected the Indian Institute of Science (Bangalore), the National Physical Laboratory (Delhi), and the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR) laboratories. The Partition Council’s Scientific Committee recommended that research projects with cross‑border relevance — such as the Indus basin hydrology studies — be continued under a bilateral protocol. An early Indo‑Pakistan agreement on scientific cooperation provided for the exchange of seismic data and collaboration on Himalayan glacier monitoring. This scientific partition, though less visible than the division of railways or the armed forces, established a precedent for technical cooperation that persisted despite political tensions.

  • Confusing the Gandhi‑Irwin Pact’s limited concessions with a full settlement of the Civil Disobedience Movement; the salt tax remained and the demand for Purna Swaraj was not met.
  • Assuming the 1935 Act’s federal scheme was implemented; the federation never came into force because princely states did not accede in sufficient numbers.
  • Overstating the Quit India Movement’s immediate effect on army recruitment; the shortfall was largely compensated by expanded recruitment and non‑combatant labour corps by 1944.
  • Attributing the 1946 RIN mutiny solely to the INA trials; racial discrimination, pay grievances, and post‑war demobilisation fears were equally significant.
  • Believing the Indian Meteorological Department was split evenly; the majority of observatories and staff remained with India, and Pakistan inherited a smaller but functional network.
  • Neglecting the voluntary‑option principle for scientific personnel, which mirrored the civil services but is often omitted in textbook summaries.
  • Treating early Indo‑Pakistan scientific agreements as a one‑off; they laid the groundwork for continued data exchange on seismology and hydrology.

The Federal Scheme of the 1935 Act: Princely State Accession, the Chamber of Princes, and the Failure of Federation

The Government of India Act 1935 envisaged an All‑India Federation that would unite the eleven British Indian provinces with the princely states on a voluntary basis. Accession required each ruler to sign an Instrument of Accession accepting the federal legislature’s authority over defence, external affairs, communications and a few other subjects. The Act created the Chamber of Princes as a consultative body to represent the states in the federal legislature; its members were nominated by the rulers and could debate but not vote on federal bills. For the federation to come into force, at least half the states representing half the total population of the states had to accede — a threshold never reached. By 1939 only a handful of states, notably Mysore, Travancore and Baroda, had signed instruments, while the larger states such as Hyderabad, Jammu & Kashmir and Bhopal held back, fearing loss of sovereignty and the Congress’s republican stance. The Congress, which won the 1937 provincial elections, opposed the federal scheme because it preserved British paramountcy and gave the princes a veto over constitutional progress. The 1939 resignation of Congress ministries over the war declaration further stalled any federal negotiation. The 1942 Cripps Mission offered dominion status after the war but retained the federal framework, which the Congress rejected. The 1946 Cabinet Mission proposed a three‑tier federation with a weak centre, but the Muslim League’s demand for grouping and the Congress’s insistence on a strong centre led to deadlock. Ultimately the 1947 Mountbatten Plan abandoned the federal idea altogether, partitioning British India and leaving the princely states to accede individually to either dominion. The failure of the 1935 federal scheme thus shaped the post‑war constitutional trajectory: it demonstrated that a voluntary federation of unequal units was politically unworkable and cleared the way for a sovereign, unitary Constitution in 1950.

The 1935 Act's Central Dyarchy: Governor‑General's Special Responsibilities, Safeguards, and the Limits of Executive Authority

The 1935 Act retained dyarchy at the centre, dividing subjects into reserved and transferred lists. Reserved subjects — defence, external affairs, ecclesiastical affairs and tribal areas — remained under the direct control of the Governor‑General, who exercised them through his Executive Council of British officials. Transferred subjects — education, health, agriculture, local self‑government — were placed under Indian ministers responsible to the Federal Legislature. The Governor‑General possessed “special responsibilities” to safeguard minority rights, the rights of Indian states, the integrity of the civil services and the financial stability of the federation. He could certify any bill passed by the legislature if he deemed it contrary to these responsibilities, and he could issue ordinances during legislative recess. The Act also embedded safeguards: the Secretary of State for India retained control over the Indian Civil Service appointments, and the British Parliament could disallow any federal law. In practice, the 1937‑39 Congress ministries operated only in the provincial sphere; the centre remained a British preserve. The 1942 Cripps Mission attempted to replace dyarchy with a fully responsible executive, but the offer was rejected. The 1946 Interim Government, formed under the Cabinet Mission plan, functioned under the Governor‑General’s executive authority, with the Viceroy retaining the defence portfolio. Only the Indian Independence Act 1947 abolished central dyarchy, transferring all powers to the new dominion governments. The dyarchic structure thus acted as a constitutional brake, ensuring that even after provincial autonomy the ultimate executive authority rested with the Crown until the final transfer of power.

Examiner's technique
A question asks: “Explain how the Governor‑General’s special responsibilities under the 1935 Act limited the scope of responsible government at the centre.”
Identify the reserved subjects (defence, external affairs, ecclesiastical affairs, tribal areas) that remained under the Governor‑General’s exclusive control. Note the certification power: the Governor‑General could refuse assent to any federal bill that threatened minority safeguards, princely rights, civil‑service integrity or financial stability. Mention the ordinance‑making power during legislative recess. Conclude that these powers meant Indian ministers could not control key portfolios and the legislature’s laws could be overridden, so responsible government was confined to transferred subjects only.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for “limits of responsible government” under the 1935 Act, structure the answer in three layers: (1) reserved subjects, (2) Governor‑General’s special responsibilities and certification, (3) British parliamentary override. Use the phrase “dyarchy at the centre” to signal the constitutional concept. Avoid conflating provincial dyarchy (1919 Act) with central dyarchy (1935 Act).

The Post‑Trial Release of INA Prisoners and the Political Negotiations of 1946‑47

The Indian National Army trials held at the Red Fort between November 1945 and May 1946 generated massive public sympathy. The Congress, the Muslim League and the Communist Party organised demonstrations demanding the release of the accused officers — Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Sahgal and Gurbaksh Singh Dhillon. In January 1946 the British announced the release of the three principal accused, but the remaining 20,000 INA personnel remained in detention camps. The 1946 provincial elections, held under the 1935 Act, turned into a referendum on the INA issue; Congress candidates campaigned on the promise of complete release. After the elections, the newly formed Interim Government under Jawaharlal Nehru pressed the Viceroy, Lord Wavell, for a general amnesty. Wavell, advised by the Commander‑in‑Chief, agreed to a phased release: first the officers, then the rank‑and‑file, conditional on their undertaking not to engage in political activity. The release was completed by early 1947. The political impact was twofold: it legitimised the INA as a nationalist force, allowing former soldiers to join the Congress and the Muslim League, and it weakened the British military’s moral authority, a factor cited in the 1946 Royal Indian Navy mutiny. The British used the release as a bargaining chip during the Cabinet Mission negotiations, arguing that concessions on the INA demonstrated goodwill. However, the Congress insisted that the release be unconditional, and the League demanded parity for Muslim INA veterans. The episode illustrated how a military‑legal issue became a lever in the final constitutional negotiations.

The Partition of the Survey of India: Map Custody, Geodetic Data, and the 1947 Boundary Demarcation

The Survey of India, founded in 1767, held the definitive topographic and geodetic records of the subcontinent, including the Great Trigonometrical Survey triangulation network, cadastral maps, and theodolite observations. At partition the Radcliffe Line required precise, large‑scale maps for the Punjab and Bengal boundaries. The Partition Council established a Survey Committee chaired by the Surveyor General of India. The committee allocated map sheets on a territorial basis: sheets covering West Punjab, Sind, North‑West Frontier Province and East Bengal were transferred to Pakistan; the remainder stayed with India. The headquarters at Dehradun, the central map repository, the geodetic computing centre, and the instrument workshops remained in India. Pakistan received the Lahore and Peshawar field offices, together with their field books and triangulation data for the Indus basin. The committee arranged for the duplication of the primary triangulation records (1901‑1947) so that both dominions could maintain a continuous geodetic framework. A joint technical sub‑committee continued to exchange survey data for border pillar verification until 1950. The division also covered the Survey’s library of historical maps, which was split by region. The 1947 demarcation teams used Survey of India 1‑inch‑to‑1‑mile sheets to lay down boundary pillars; discrepancies in the field led to the 1948‑49 Indo‑Pakistan boundary commissions. The partition of the Survey thus created two independent national survey organisations — the Survey of India and the Survey of Pakistan — each inheriting a functional geodetic infrastructure, a precedent for technical cooperation that survived political hostility.

The Partition of the Indian Postal Savings Bank and the Post Office Life Insurance Fund: Asset Division and Continuity

The Post Office Savings Bank (POSB) and the Post Office Life Insurance Fund (POLIF) were the largest retail financial institutions in British India, with over 12 million depositors and policies worth several hundred crores of rupees. The Partition Council’s Financial Committee, assisted by the Reserve Bank of India, devised a domicile‑based division: accounts and policies held in post offices located in the territories assigned to Pakistan (West Punjab, Sind, NWFP, East Bengal, Baluchistan) were transferred to the newly created Pakistan Post Office Savings Bank and Pakistan Post Office Life Insurance Fund. The transfer involved physical movement of ledger books, passbooks and policy registers, supervised by joint Indian‑Pakistani audit teams. The Reserve Bank acted as the clearing house for the settlement of inter‑dominion balances, crediting the Pakistan entities with the rupee value of transferred deposits and debiting the Indian POSB accordingly. The 1948 India‑Pakistan Monetary Agreement provided for the conversion of these balances into sterling at the agreed exchange rate, ensuring that depositors suffered no loss of value. Both dominions retained the existing interest‑rate structure and the agency network; the Indian POSB continued under the Department of Posts, while Pakistan established its own postal savings administration. The division set a precedent for the bifurcation of retail financial institutions without disrupting small‑saver confidence, and the POSB/POLIF model remained the backbone of financial inclusion in both countries for decades.

  • Confusing the 1935 Act’s federal scheme with the 1919 dyarchy; the federation never operated, while provincial dyarchy functioned from 1921.
  • Assuming the Governor‑General’s special responsibilities were merely advisory; they included statutory certification and ordinance powers that could override the legislature.
  • Believing all INA prisoners were released immediately after the Red Fort trials; the release was phased and conditional, completing only in early 1947.
  • Thinking the Survey of India’s maps were split evenly; the majority of the geodetic infrastructure and headquarters remained with India.
  • Overlooking the domicile principle in the POSB/POLIF division; accounts followed the post office’s location, not the depositor’s religion.
  • Neglecting the role of the Reserve Bank as the clearing agent for inter‑dominion financial settlements during partition.
  • Treating the 1947 boundary demarcation as a purely political act; it relied on Survey of India’s scientific triangulation data.

The 1919 Government of India Act: Dyarchy in the Provinces and Its Operational Record (1921‑1937)

The Montagu‑Chelmsford Reforms, enacted as the Government of India Act 1919, introduced a system of dyarchy in eight major provinces. Under dyarchy, provincial subjects were divided into transferred subjects — administered by Indian ministers responsible to the legislative council — and reserved subjects — kept under the direct control of the governor and his executive council. Transferred subjects included education, public health, agriculture, and local self‑government; reserved subjects comprised law and order, finance, land revenue, and irrigation.

The first elections under the 1919 Act were held in 1920, producing legislative councils with a majority of elected Indian members. The Indian National Congress initially boycotted the councils, but the Swaraj Party, formed in 1923, contested elections and entered the councils to obstruct colonial legislation from within. In provinces such as Madras, Bombay, and the Central Provinces, Swarajist ministers worked to expand primary education, promote vernacular languages, and launch public health initiatives.

Despite these gains, dyarchy suffered from structural flaws. The governor retained overriding powers, including the ability to certify bills rejected by the council and to assume control of transferred subjects during emergencies. Financial autonomy was limited because the provincial budget required the governor’s assent, and the bulk of revenue — land revenue, customs, and income tax — remained with the centre. The Simon Commission (1927) and the subsequent Round Table Conferences highlighted these deficiencies, leading to the Government of India Act 1935, which replaced dyarchy with provincial autonomy.

Dyarchy gave a generation of Indian administrators experience in legislative procedure, budgeting, and departmental management. However, the system also deepened communal tensions because separate electorates for Muslims, Sikhs, and other minorities were retained, reinforcing identity‑based politics. The 1937 provincial elections, held under the 1935 Act, marked the end of dyarchy, but the administrative practices and political lessons of the 1921‑1937 period informed the functioning of the first Congress ministries.

The 1935 Act's Federal Scheme and the Princely States' Negotiations: The Failed Federation

The Government of India Act 1935 envisaged an all‑India federation comprising British Indian provinces and the princely states. The federal legislature was to be bicameral, with the Council of States representing the units and the Federal Assembly representing the population. Crucially, accession to the federation was voluntary for the princely states; each ruler had to sign an Instrument of Accession specifying the subjects — defence, external affairs, communications, and ancillary matters — that would be transferred to the federal centre.

Negotiations began in 1936 under the aegis of the Viceroy, Lord Linlithgow, and the Chancellor of the Chamber of Princes. The larger states — Hyderabad, Mysore, Kashmir, Baroda, and Gwalior — demanded guarantees for their internal autonomy, protection of their revenues, and representation proportionate to their size. The British offered a Federal Court to adjudicate disputes and a Federal Railway Authority to manage strategic lines, but refused to concede fiscal sovereignty or the right to secede.

The outbreak of the Second World War in September 1939 halted the process. The Viceroy declared India a belligerent without consulting Indian leaders, and the Congress ministries resigned in protest. The princely states, wary of a weakened British centre and the rising nationalist tide, withheld their Instruments of Accession. By 1940, few states had acceded, and the federal provisions of the 1935 Act never came into force. The federation remained a paper construct, and the 1947 partition rendered the scheme obsolete.

The failure of the federal scheme demonstrated the limits of British constitutional engineering when confronted with divergent interests of the princely order and the nationalist movement. It also set the stage for the post‑war integration of states through the Instrument of Accession under the Indian Independence Act 1947, which bypassed the elaborate federal framework altogether.

Technique: Comparative Constitutional Analysis
Compare the federal provisions of the 1935 Act with the post‑1947 integration of princely states. Identify two structural reasons why the 1935 federation failed while the 1947 accession succeeded.
The 1935 federation required voluntary accession by each princely state to a central legislature where they would be a permanent minority; the British retained paramountcy and refused to cede fiscal control, making the deal unattractive. In 1947, the Indian Independence Act transferred paramountcy to the new dominions, and the Instrument of Accession covered only three subjects (defence, external affairs, communications), leaving internal autonomy intact. The political context — British withdrawal, communal violence, and the clear choice between India and Pakistan — forced rapid decisions, whereas the 1935 scheme lacked urgency and offered no credible security guarantee.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “reasons for the failure of the 1935 federal scheme”, structure the answer around three pillars: (1) voluntary accession without incentives, (2) British retention of paramountcy and financial control, (3) the disruptive impact of the Second World War. Use the 1947 accession as a contrast only if the question explicitly demands comparison.

The Indian National Army's Formation, Subhas Chandra Bose's Leadership, and Its Military Campaigns in Southeast Asia (1942‑1945)

The Indian National Army (INA) originated from Indian prisoners of war captured by the Japanese after the fall of Singapore in February 1942. Rash Behari Bose, an expatriate revolutionary, formed the first INA in 1942 under Japanese auspices, but it remained a modest force until Subhas Chandra Bose arrived in Tokyo in May 1943.
